# Stop! In the Name of Dove

A string of confusing US inflation reports argued to retain solvency despite irrationality. Price action hit our stop-out level, and we turn neutral on US duration but maintain a bullish stance in Europe. We also suggest positioning for a lower EUR/USD via options, especially to hedge equity risk.

## Global Macro Strategy

We discuss recently-released confusing US economic data and why the Fed's reaction to it will ultimately determine how investors should react. We also discuss why we think EUR/USD puts are an attractive and cheap buy to hedge for various outcomes, particularly as an overlay to long-equity portfolios.

## Interest Rate Strategy

We turn neutral on UST duration on curves. We pay maintain short Jun 24 1y midcurve 97.875/98.250 strangle; we close RX/UB invoice spread steepener box; we maintain receive EUR 5y5y (vs 6m) and maintain EUR 5y5 vs 20y5y steepener. In cash, we close our structural short 10y BTP versus Bund. We maintain receive May'24 MPC and long UKT 1T37 vs. short UKT 1T57, and we also keep our short 15y ASW. We maintain receive 2y OIS, long 20y JGBs on 10s20s30s fly, and sell 30y JGB ASW.

## Currency & Foreign Exchange

Buy EUR/USD puts to hedge an FX regime change, particularly as an overlay to long-equity portfolios. We remain neutral USD with a bullish skew, and discuss how the magnitude of the USD's reaction to CPI was larger than historical evidence on the USD's response to upside surprises in inflation data suggests. We dig into Swiss inflation and growth data to find the balance of probabilities seem to lie with a first SNB cut in June rather than March, We recommend long AUD/NZD into the February RBNZ meeting. We assess the potential risk of FX intervention for JPY.

## Inflation-Linked Bonds

We stay neutral on TIPS breakevens. We look at the seasonal performance of breakevens in February and its link to January CPI surprises.

## Short-Duration Strategy

We maintain short 2y swap spreads discuss the pause in MMF extensions and the contribution of the rates sell-off to soft funding conditions.

## Interest Rate Derivatives

We enter long 1x2x1 3m2y receiver butterfly (ATMF-10/ATMF-30/ATMF-60) as way of capturing rich receiver skew, while keeping some exposure to our strategic bullish view. As we are nearing first delivery date, we estimate fair value for the WN contract's wild-card option using swaption vol.

## The Devil You Know Is Better Than The Data You Don't

The devil is in the details. Or is it in the data? Macro markets reacted with vigor to the notable upside surprises in US inflation data. On the back of the CPI report, our economists forecasted January core PCE at 0.29% M/M. Then after the PPI report, they revised that forecast higher, to 0.43% M/M.

The forecast increase to 0.43% M/M when compared to their pre-CPI forecast of 0.23% M/ M, indicates that they now believe the economy produced nearly twice as much inflation in January than they thought before. The January CPI and PPI inflation reports suggest something more rocky than the "bumpy" inflation path previously suggested.

How much weight should investors put on this data? The answer depends on how much weight FOMC participants place on it. After all, the way in which FOMC participants reacted to activity data in 3Q23 – despite encouraging inflation data – sent US Treasury yields sharply higher (see The Real Drivers of the US Treasury Bond Market Rout).

We encourage FOMC participants, and thus investors, to take this inflation data with a grain of salt. When easily identifiable divergences occur within the inflation basket, skeptics should emerge. For example, owner's equivalent rent (OER) increased by 0.56% M/M, while rents or primary residences (rents) increased 0.36% M/M (see Exhibit 1 ).

The monthly increase in OER relative to the monthly increase in rents represented a 4 standard deviation divergency when analyzing monthly data for the past decade (see Exhibit 2 ). If the errors are normally distributed, this should only occur once out of 15,787 times. If BLS released CPI daily, this would only occur twice in a lifetime (every 43 years).

Exhibit 1: US CPI rent of primary residence M/M vs. owner’s equivalent rent M/M
![](images/e257f5f67513ac6ebaaf5bde732b230916120015cd07d4a4409141e564d17eac.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, BLS, Bloomberg

Exhibit 2: Standard deviation of residual: US CPI rent of primary residence vs. owner’s equivalent rent
![](images/c8360394391bd80c9dee3d65dbaa0aa7f7890c275b5d59f22d1fa39b9ef03776.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, BLS, Bloomberg

The relative historical volatilities of rents and OER raise additional questions. Over most of the past 20 years, the volatility of monthly rent inflation surpassed the volatility of monthly OER inflation. This relationship sustained itself during the pandemic when volatility in both increased dramatically (see Exhibit 3 ).

However, over the past year, monthly volatility in rents and OER largely returned to prepandemic levels. During this time, volatility in monthly rent inflation came in higher than OER again, but by a wider margin than before the pandemic (see Exhibit 4 ). That places the January increase in OER at odds with the more tame increase in rents.

Exhibit 3: 4-year rolling standard deviation of M/M changes in rents and OER prices
![](images/5527dac8e35fe3c8e981ad2eedd251307d6cfd498e40d66a939c4ced5bb43320.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, BLS, Bloomberg
Exhibit 4: Standard deviation of M/M changes in rents and OER prices over different periods

![](images/a4fbeea35194a138bfd6ac83f9d7a6ef06443b05d7d50541323e027ff052a247.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, BLS, Bloomberg

Our economists and housing strategists have good reason to expect OER inflation to remain higher, or stickier, than rent inflation. They cite the following explanations for a slower deceleration in OER inflation than rent inflation:

1. Single-family rental units have a higher weight in OER than before (see Recent and upcoming methodology changes: 2022).

2. New tenant rental inflation leads renewal tenant rental inflation, which then leads OER (see Exhibit 5 , and Disentangling Rent Index Differences: Data, Methods, and Scope).

3. Single-family rental units turn over less frequently (fewer new tenants) than multifamily rental units.

4. When the labor market cools, rent inflation falls faster than OER inflation, as it has done recently (see Exhibit 6 ).

We can also use these explanations to understand (1) why rent inflation may be more volatile, especially as it slows, than OER; and (2) what a higher rate of rent disinflation compared to OER implies about the labor market, i.e., the labor market is cooling, despite what the January nonfarm payroll report may have suggested.

Exhibit 5: New tenant repeat rent (NTRR) Y/Y leads all tenant repeat rent (ATRR) Y/Y leads owner's equivalent rent (OER) Y/Y
![](images/68d991dde17ad8d34533c55b683ef7fe4e5a02699320213b117e2a69c774bcc8.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, FRB Cleveland, Bloomberg

Exhibit 6: All tenant repeat rent (ATRR) Y/Y leads CPI owners equivalent rent (OER) Y/Y
![](images/f3da7734ff766bb3f080ebaba169f4fb43e39b7d87dcd4f6f635ceaa8761ae35.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, BLS, Bloomberg

Even with the appropriate discounting of housing-related inflation in January, investors must confront the acceleration in services inflation ex-housing (see Exhibit 7 ). Are they meant to discount that as well? Putting aside the highly questionable January data point – the largest monthly jump in services ex-housing prices in 18 months – it depends.

Spending on services – or goods for that matter – comes from income, credit, or savings. We don't see how labor market income growth created outsized demand for services in 2H23 (see Exhibit 8 ). Nor do we see how an expansion of credit provision by banks helped consumers spend more on services (see Exhibit 9 ).

Exhibit 7: Core CPI services ex-housing M/M SAAR, 6-month annualized
![](images/1d08f41fdee412f637c2c26dddaf893b29b2800bf909a8c87edcf0295f853e58.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, BLS, Bloomberg

Exhibit 8: US private nonfarm payrolls income proxy M/M SAAR, 6-month annualized
![](images/5826d31d0a3eec22bc5482d857437d00b9a0cbf3afa54eebfe4d0f1ba34c5de0.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, BLS, Bloomberg

That leaves savings as the source of spending, which lines up with the decline in the savings rate in 2H23 (see Exhibit 10 ). If savings drove consumption of services higher, which led to higher price inflation, we would question its sustainable nature.

Exhibit 9: US commercial bank lending to consumers: All loans, credit card/revolving, 6m SAAR
![](images/904400fa194b2e120c0b425af16cc6452abeb81d76c9fcbb3e306b6f3341f2b4.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Federal Reserve, Bloomberg

Exhibit 10: US personal savings as a % of disposable income
![](images/9ab81afd3b3af35263fc1577f109a126ab8cf8be1730e27e431672ee92876684.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, BEA, Bloomberg

What should investors make of this? The confusing nature of recent economic data suggest investors should interpret the data as the Fed would. Our US economists don't think recent data changed the views of FOMC participants. As such, we suggest investors move to a neutral stance on US duration, in an effort to remain solvent while the irrationality of the data passes by.

## The Hedge of Reason in G10 FX

We think EUR/USD puts are an attractive and cheap buy to hedge for various outcomes, particularly as an overlay to long-equity portfolios. We recommend 1y EUR/USD puts around at-the-money spot. For full details, see G10 FX Strategy: The Hedge of Reason, February 16, 2024.

## • Trade idea: Buy EUR/USD 1y put near at-the-money-spot (ATMS, 1.07) for 1.76%

Price action YTD has largely corresponded with regime 2 of our four-regime USD framework. At a high level, US real rates and breakevens have moved higher, generating both a stronger USD and higher equities (see Exhibit 11 ).

Intra-asset rotations are also playing ball. Oil is outperforming gold, cyclical equity baskets are outperforming defensives (see Exhibit 12 ), and in FX, low yielders are underperforming risk-sensitive currencies (see Exhibit 13 ). USD has gained more than history suggests, though.

Exhibit 11: YTD price action has been firmly in regime 2 of our four-regime USD framework percent
![](images/d298467b1e783fd204eced510efd4ab0992065a143a85b70d06aca79488c5033.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 12: Cross-asset price action has largely conformed to historical regime 2 patterns...

![](images/5b8ebc38b864668c3088f6eed8b8f6c4987e803103583cf3466ece85f0bf8e7c.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Why? Because investors are left with the 'what to buy' problem. Investors remain skeptical of growth prospects in Europe and China, and the limited currencies on the 'buy' list (largely high carry EMs) are seen as both crowded and consensus. 2024 election risks have amplified investor reluctance to sell USD.

Exhibit 13: ...though USD has gained more than predicted
![](images/eefd4d4f24b383afe718c814f57db8ece974e47afe039459cf8009329f4dbbe6.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 14: USD has become increasingly decorrelated to equity movements
![](images/07ef706c1f30d0fd5ceb4c2e2884db57159eaf00d856d6b6c7c7bed776c63d19.jpg)

An interesting consequence of these dynamics is that changes in USD have become increasingly decorrelated to changes in equities (see Exhibit 14 ). We think this uncorrelated dynamic is both temporary and advantageous: it suggests that long USD could be an important – ­ and positive-carrying – hedge for long equity portfolios.

We think this hedge is attractive in large part because of its versatility – it should cover the investor in a variety of different scenarios. We summarize how we expect equities, EUR/USD, and the components of the EUR/USD 1y put to perform in each of the regimes in Exhibit 15 .

Exhibit 15: How do EUR/USD and EUR/USD optionality perform in different regime scenarios?
<table><tr><td></td><td>Regime 1</td><td>Regime 2</td><td>Regime 3</td><td>Regime 4</td></tr><tr><td>10y Real Yield</td><td>↓</td><td>↑</td><td>↑</td><td>↓</td></tr><tr><td>10y Breakeven</td><td>↑</td><td>↑</td><td>↓</td><td>↓</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3">Average Monthly Return (bp)</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>S&amp;P 500</td><td>191</td><td>242</td><td>-59</td><td>-116</td></tr><tr><td>USD/EUR</td><td>-127</td><td>6</td><td>78</td><td>23</td></tr><tr><td>FXVIX</td><td>-26</td><td>-40</td><td>30</td><td>30</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3">EUR/USD ATMS 1y Put Return Contributions</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Spot</td><td>一</td><td>+</td><td>+</td><td>+</td></tr><tr><td>Forward Points</td><td>+</td><td>一</td><td>一</td><td>+</td></tr><tr><td>FX Volatility</td><td>一</td><td>_</td><td>+</td><td>+</td></tr></table>

Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research; Note: For forward points foreign yields are assumed to be unchanged versus falling or rising US yields.

Exhibit 16: Implied EUR/USD volatility has fallen, rendering options cheaper to buy
![](images/4c83367080d37242eaedd59206c9436900a82eb997fe979d7b4cb842a1d7025c.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

If we stay in regime 2, equities rise but EUR/USD tends to fall a bit, with the spot move helping to offset the lost premium from time decay and the move in forwards. But moving to regimes 3 or 4 sees USD rise and volatility pick up.

Even a shift to regime 1, arguably the worst outcome for USD, sees the loss in option premium from spot and volatility partly offset by a flatter forward curve (and of course, higher equities).

Given the historical cheapness of EUR/USD volatility (see Exhibit 16 ), and the potential for EUR/USD to serve as an effective US election hedge, we think long-term EUR/USD puts could be an important and helpful overlay hedge to a risk-positive portfolio.

## Our Current Stance on Markets

In global rates markets, we close long 5y UST. We enter Long 1x2x1 3m2y receiver butterfly ATMF-10bp/ATMF-35bp/ATMF-60bp (\$300mm notional on long legs). We maintain short 2y SOFR swap spread, long 10s on 5s10s30s butterfly, and long Feb44 iota.

In the euro area, we close short Bund ASW vs. Buxl ASW box, short EU 3.25% 2034 on ASW, buy EU 0.7% 2051 vs. EU 2.5% 2052, and short BTP 4.40% May ’33 vs. Bund 1.7% Aug ’32. We enter short SPGB 3.25% Apr 2034 vs. SPGB 3.5% May 2029 and SPGB 1.9% Oct 2052. We maintain receive EUR 5y5y (vs. 6m) and short 0RM4 97.875/98.250 strangle. We continue to recommend buy RXH4 136/137/138.5 broken call fly, receive EUR 5y5y swap (vs. 6m) versus EUR 20y5y swap (vs. 6m), and long OATei31.

In the dollar bloc, we maintain pay April 2024 BoC and receive June 2025 BoC. We continue to recommend New Zealand OIS 2s10s steepener and Australia OIS 2s10s flattener.

In foreign exchange markets, we enter long AUD/NZD (target: 1.12, stop: 1.04). We maintain short CAD/NOK (target: 7.00, stop: 8.25), long NOK/SEK (target: 1.06, stop: 0.95), and short EUR/JPY (target: 145, stop: 162).

In FX options, we enter long EUR/USD 1y put near at-the-money spot (ATMS, 1.07). We maintain 1y USD/CHF 10-delta strangle (0.7775 put, 0.9550 call).

# Interest Rate Strategy

## United States

Stopped out, staying tactically neutral: Last week, we suggested staying long 5y UST, with a stop-out at 4.24%, recognizing risks from strong data and upside momentum. That stop-out was breached this week after the CPI print, automatically turning us neutral on duration. While we see some role of seasonal impacts in the data, we do not see a narrative-changing data point until the next payroll report 3 weeks from now, keeping us tactically neutral on duration and curves.

Are seasonal effects helping data outperform? January core CPI has surprised to the upside in 11 of the last 12 years, reminding us of the residual seasonality that BEA attempted to fix. Additionally, the payroll strength in January may have been helped by a very negative January seasonal adjustment in a labor market has favored labor hoarding and thus lower layoffs.

Seasonal data = seasonal Treasuries? Helped by residual seasonality in January data, Treasury yields are now starting to show a high degree of upside seasonality in February. 5y Treasury yields have risen in 12 of the last 16 Februaries, and 6 of the last 7 Februaries (with 2020 being the exception). We also see that the statistical significance of seasonality in February has breached the 95% confidence level over the last five years.

Should investors believe that OER uptick or cooling rents? We show that (1) rents tend to fall sooner than OER given a higher fraction of multifamily housing included in rents, and (2) OER volatility is inversely linked to utility price inflation. Given falling rents and cooling utility prices, both these factors have widened the OER-rent wedge. The big-picture focus for investors should be on the cooling rent rather and the uptick in volatile OER.

Adding 1x2x1 receiver spreads: We suggest adding a 1x2x1 3m2y receiver butterfly (ATMF -10/ ATMF-30/ATMF-60) trade - cost 3bp running - as way of monetizing the rich receiver skew while keeping some exposure to our strategic bullish view.

## Euro area

Over the past two weeks, the Bund has been predominantly driven by Treasuries, with little data released in Europe and ECB speakers less in the spotlight. There is a strong correlation between Bunds and Treasuries; however, we think the questions are 1) How long can the correlation stay that high? and 2) Can strong US data – should positive surprises continue – translate into wider 10y UST-Bund spreads?

The outlook for Europe in terms of inflation and growth hasn’t changed meaningfully, and structural divergences between the US and Europe remain. Thus, despite a high correlation between 10y Bund and 10y Treasuries, we believe Bunds are an attractive vehicle to be long duration given the macro and valuations. Price action could remain rangy for the next two months, according to the Bund fair value path, which should favour trading duration more tactically than structurally for now.

Regarding ASW, the Bund/Buxl ASW box steepened 3bp+ in one week, which represents roughly a one-week standard deviation; hence, we close the trade. For investors who wish to position for cheaper core ASW via long SSAs vs. Bunds or OATs, we think CADES and EFSF could be attractive from a supply perspective.

The resumption of the BTP/Bund spread this week makes short EU 34s ASW less attractive, in our opinion, and hence we close the trade. We also close the EU 51s52s micro steepeners. The slope steepened back recently but less than expected as it failed to follow the steepening of OATs 30s50s.

Regarding cash EGBs, we highlight how our model is pointing to a rich 10y Spain within the 5s10s30s fly. We like playing for a cheapening of the 10y Spain in the fly as a tactical trade, whilst on BTP/Bund, we highlight how the some of the assumptions behind our widening thesis into 1Q have now changed. All in all, given the observed tightening and considering the ample room that foreign investors have in order to add on BTPs, we close our wideners as receding supply pressures, potential strong demand from retails in the upcoming BTP Valore issuance, and the 10y spread still flagging as cheap vs credit could lead to some other tightening in the spread.

## United Kingdom

Last week of data were mixed overall with a beat in wage growth numbers offset by a downside surprise in the January inflation prints. Overall, the January Inflation Report supports a normalization in monetary policy in the short term, in our view, and we think the BoE could start cutting rates as early as in May.

That said, the beat in the December pay growth poses hawkish risks alongside the upcoming budget, and more importantly, the risk of a re-acceleration in inflation, which might continue to take out curve inversion globally. With these considerations, we keep our receiving May'24 MPC given the limited downside risks, while we think that cross-market trades are starting to look attractive as the last inflation print confirms that UK inflation is unlikely to have any idiosyncratic risk.

Looking ahead, supply will likely again take center stage as the reopening of the 4 63s tap is expected on Tuesday (£1.75bn nominal and \~£3m/bp DV01); this is an important test on ultras demand. Meanwhile, the linker syndication is expected in March (possibly the week starting in March 11), which should bring a decent amount of DV01 (\~£10 m/bp assuming a new 30y linker will be issued). In terms of market implications, we think the linker syndication could add re-steepening pressure both in real yield and nominal space.

## Japan

With the market seemingly seeing a March or April BoJ hike as almost a done deal, the focus of investor attention continued to be the (as yet highly uncertain) longerterm rate hike trajectory. Markets are trying to assess the future policy rates trajectory, in response to incoming data and the change in the external environment.

We think mixed US data as well as weak GDP prints (particularly capex investment and household consumption) make it difficult to position for a sharper normalization path. Hence, we prefer trades with a bullish bias for duration.

However, with the belly of the curve increasingly volatile depending on incoming data/the external environment, we prefer any bullish duration exposure on the wings rather than on the belly of the curve.

We maintain receive 2y OIS for its attractive carry + rolldown. We also continue to see value in long 20y JGBs on 10s20s30s fly, since the 20y sector looks cheaper vs fair value, and 20y tends to outperform on the curve in any rally. We are also sticking with our 30y JGB ASW short recommendation as a means of focusing on supply/demand headwinds in the 30y JGB sector.

## Stopped out at 4.24% 5s, tactically neutral for now

Last week, we noted that the upside momentum in yields and the strong payroll data had increased the risk that yields could keep rising. We suggested staying long 5y UST at 4.09% last week, with a stop-out at 4.24%. That stop out was breached this week (see Exhibit 17 ) after the CPI print, automatically turning us neutral on duration.

While we see some role of seasonal impacts in the data, we do not see a narrativechanging data point until the next payroll report 3 weeks from now – keeping us tactically neutral on duration and curves.

Exhibit 17: Moves in yields and breakevens over the last week
![](images/2eb53846d7a50db03ffbe7eb2b3eed1ad85213838fa37303b0d008b576596795.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 18: Probability of rate cuts through December 2024 FOMC meeting
![](images/2e0d1dd5d99b59cff5caffdab6e9cca79a1cab3730d52e782bfb0debb5531456.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

We understand the strong optics from economic data - with payrolls and CPI strength being complemented by PPI and ISM surveys. However, investors must bear in mind the possible seasonal distortions in the data. Interestingly, markets were able to discount the weak retail sales on the back of seasonal distortions – our economists explicitly suggested those – and we think markets have shown less appreciation for possible seasonal distortions in CPI and payrolls, which we discuss below.

Importantly, the possible role of seasonals in January data reminds us of the issue of residual seasonality (higher inflation,lower growth in 1Q) that has long been a known issue with US data. While the Covid years of 2020 and 2021 muddied the 1Q data enough to cast doubts on whether residual seasonality persisted, the last 2-3 years of data is raising that possibility again.

Exhibit 19: 5y Treasury yield moves in February in the last 25 years
![](images/93c4c802cc766dbdc85671729763edf602c658b6aa791962bfc59e76dbfc0003.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 20: Confidence for statistically significant February seasonality by history window
![](images/c595ddb98b3b330c2913d8804bbf9fa0d757234aac35ac61bff236fd7ee5c43d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Helped by residual seasonality in January data, Treasury yields are now starting to show a high degree of upside seasonality in February, when January data is released. 5y Treasury yields have risen in 12 of the last 16 Februaries, and 6 of the last 7 Februaries (with 2020 being the exception). We also see that the statistical significance of seasonality in February has breached the 95% confidence level over the last five years, suggesting this seasonality trend has become more pronounced despite efforts to eliminate residual seasonality in the data.

Specifically on the seasonality of the inflation data, 11 of the last 12 January core CPI prints have surprised to the upside. Similarly, trimmed mean CPI has formed a local peak in the month of January in five of the last seven years (see Exhibit 22 ), another sign pointing to the possibility of residual seasonality-driven upside in January CPI prints.

Exhibit 21: Core CPI surprises over the last 12 January months
![](images/ec5f76cead6f312ed30fda1949b546ef86a97eabe182ae570e0078df285ec4a3.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 22: Trimmed mean CPI in the last seven years
![](images/9700ada6ad19f6d48701725a6c0edf46c2b9e9dfaf76e453cf199389fa76a7ea.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

On the seasonality in the payroll data, as we noted two weeks ago, the January seasonal adjustment of negative 3 million implies that the month of January is more about layoffs than hiring (see Exhibit 23 ). And given that there has been a tendency for labor hoarding in the US labor market, it is perhaps unsurprising that January payrolls showed fewer layoffs than seasonals implied (i.e., a high seasonally adjusted payroll number).

Exhibit 23: Monthly seasonal adjustment for private payrolls
![](images/87db8c5fc4f713ea39fa29414c0617f7bf923d381030dff64b422b43125b34a7.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 24: Monthly rent and OER prints over the last 2 years
![](images/1818bc7e032a36e6f0150bc8f43b385728a9914e18716dfd8abecf53491c3c1b.jpg)

Notable bits from strong inflation: Outside a general seasonality-driven impulse, January core CPI also contained notable tidbits on shelter inflation. Rent inflation continued to decelerate, while owners equivalent rent (OER) showed a big spike up – the biggest OERrent gap in the last decade (see Exhibit 24 ). So should investors believe the rent-iscooling story or OER-is-heating-up story?

Note that there are 2 fundamental differences between rent and OER in the way they are captured in CPI. These differences not only help explain the wide gap between OER and rents, but also suggest that the broad rent cooling trend may continue, in line with our economics team's forecast for cooling shelter inflation in 2024.

First, the sample for the OER measurement has a bigger representation from single-family homes, where housing costs are typically stickier than the "rent" category in CPI, which has higher share of multi-family apartments. This means the rent category in CPI typically moves before OER when things change - and the OER-rent spread typically moves inversely to the direction of rents. This relationship is confirmed by observing the wedge vs. rent inflation in recent years – see Exhibit 25 .

The second key difference between OER and rents is the removal of utility costs from OER, whereby OER measures the "pure rent". Said different OER = rent - utilities. Assuming market rents move gradually, and utilities are much more volatile, OER volatility will be the inverse of volatility in utilities. Indeed, we find that the OER rent wedge is inverse and lagged in relationship to utility costs - see Exhibit 26 . As utility costs dropped in the last six months, unsurprisingly the OER- rent wedge has widened.

Exhibit 25: Move in y/y OER -rent inflation wedge vs. rent inflation for the last twenty years
![](images/9418b2ff8d616fddee52637504c62945eb45e01cdb553f1c544302e5542f0704.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 26: OER-rent inflation wedge vs. Energy services inflation in the last twenty years
![](images/70e7802b51ab404dc5b6dbfdcd9706a3d68f52b6e34a236c0fbe9ca746278cc2.jpg)

Overall, the contribution of cooling electricity and cooling rents suggests that the wide OER - wedge isn't necessarily a sign of re-accelerating inflation but is explained by the design factors behind rent and OER. And therefore the big-picture focus for investors should be on the cooling rent picture.

However, as we have noted above, we think markets will need compelling evidence to change the perception that the economy isn't re-accelerating or that inflation isn't sticky, for which markets may have to wait until the next payroll report, nearly three weeks away. Thus despite our reservations around the seasonal impact on the strong data, we suggest staying neutral on duration and curves.

Finally, we suggest adding a 1x2x1 3m2y receiver butterfly (ATMF -10/ ATMF-30/ATMF-60) trade - cost 3bp running - as way of monetizing the rich receiver skew while keeping some exposure to our strategic bullish view. See details in United States | Enter 3m2y 1x2x1 receiver butterfly .

• Trade idea: Enter 1x2x1 3m2y receiver butterfly (ATMF -10/ ATMF-30/ ATMF-60) trade @ 3bp running

• Trade idea: Closed long 5y UST at 4.24% on February 13

## Duration

Price action on the Bund over these past two weeks has been predominantly driven by Treasuries (and Gilts), with little data released in Europe and ECB speakers less in the spotlight. On the other side of the Atlantic, the Fed pivot narrative from December quickly switched in 2024 amidst a less dovish Fed meeting, strong NPFs, and reaccelerating core CPI. Retail sales this week, on the other hand, were a miss, balancing the picture.

This unsurprisingly weighed on duration both in Europe and the US and pushed the 10y UST/Bund wider by \~18bp since the NFPs (see Exhibit 28 ). Despite our view that the European landscape is calling for more of a rally (for more see here and here where we added bullish trades), investors might be concerned that Treasuries could cast a dark cloud over European duration. This week, we assess that risk.

Exhibit 29 illustrates the one-month and the three-month correlations of daily changes in the 10y Bund yields and 10y Treasury yields. The three-month correlation has been rising gradually since late October, reaching 78% last week, a high since January 2020. On the other hand, the one-month correlation, which was stable around 60/70% from mid-December to late January, crept up again to 80%+ in the last two weeks. In our opinion, this increase in correlation is sensible given the lack of idiosyncratic drivers in Europe recently.

Exhibit 28: Recent moves in European and US duration
![](images/3d1235e08c1e43393dff2f1cc506cdd587f1d3b3c71f97deb20212b88e0b3639.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 29: The 1m correlation between Bunds and USTs crept up again to 80%+ in the last two weeks
![](images/5def6a628964118525f4f207da54fff2d6244dd3e98a08fb9dd0119d9764349d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research; Note: correlation of daily changes

The strong correlations are clear; however, we think the questions are 1) How long can they stay that high? and 2) Can strong US data – should positive surprises continue – translate into wider 10y UST-Bund spreads, thus limiting the sell-off in Bunds?

In 2023, 10y UST/Bund spreads widened more than 100bp from trough to peak amidst a strong divergence in data surprises between the US and Europe. This can be seen in Exhibit 30 with the difference between the Economic Surprise Index in the US and in Europe and Exhibit 31 , which shows the levels. Since 4Q23, the gap between both series came down, which could partly explain the relatively rangy UST/Bund between 170bp and 190bp over the past months.

Exhibit 30: 10y UST/Bund spreads are hovering around 180bp as the divergence in data surprises declined
![](images/fb95341ddb7f65111b2495f1bb9712f8c2bc3eb9eaa84e3374b607ddb787844a.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 31: YTD, data in Europe and the US tended to surprise to the upside
![](images/8b0a4392e3bcdf9bb8271d1b95aa4387c2ede897502c7fd8f706320440c4f66d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Yes, European data started surprising to the upside, but this doesn’t change the macro outlook in Europe. For instance, the upside surprise in the January HICP led our economists to revise higher their headline and core inflation paths. However, the impact is minimal on our Bund FV path for 2024, with the direction still for lower rates in the medium term and a fair value decreasing to \~1.6 by YE (see Exhibit 32 ).

Similarly, PMIs were a positive surprise in January , but as our economists note, PMIs remain at a low level, confirming the risks of a 1Q24 disappointment for GDP (which they forecast at 0.1% q/q). All in all, in our view the outlook for Europe in terms of inflation and growth hasn’t changed meaningfully, and structural divergences between the US and Europe remain.

Exhibit 32: Bund FV path for 2024 – lower rates in the medium term and a fair value decreasing to \~1.6 by YE
![](images/fae3288daacf2d2c088b3c4ff85dec26d3e417c6c02882e89ace4a3e841e8324.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research estimates

Exhibit 33: Historical analysis suggests we are entering a range to rally period
![](images/b4696e345569874af3e71ed6d50017f66ff452f3a468a973718d3df804abc5fa.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

In terms of valuations, our models also suggest (i) 10y Bund yields are \~15bp cheap and (ii) 10y UST/Bund spreads are \~9bp tight. Despite correlation between 10y Bund and 10y Treasuries being high relative to the past year (and relative to previous cycles), we continue to believe Bunds are an attractive vehicle to be long duration given the macro and valuations (see Enter the Dragon).

Though we remain bullish, we reiterate that price action could remain rangy for the next two months, according to the Bund fair value path, which should favour trading duration more tactically than structurally for now. Historical analysis ( Exhibit 33 ) also suggests we are entering a range to rally period for the next month (\~20 business days).

## Positioning

The latest data from Eurex (14 Feb) shows €9.6mn/01 of shorts added in one week over the four German contracts (see Exhibit 34 ). Shorts had been reduced early February amid a deterioration of risk sentiment during a few sessions. So far in February, €15.5mn/01 of shorts have been added, mainly on the Bobl and Bund. Positioning now seems stretched in aggregate as it is edging closer to its 6m max short of €23.2mn/01 reached late September in the sell-off.

Exhibit 34: €9.6mn/01 of shorts added in one week over the four German contracts
![](images/c423a3a72cf7fb98d4d1cd6d9eb2f2e191c6671f966feee9b3cb65a1c4005939.jpg)
Source: Eurex as of 14/02/2024, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 35: Discrepancies remain between duration and positioning
![](images/cf53e16ef901387a12e7f3b0fff0581742ee2ac8454fac788e02c44f51dbe665.jpg)
Source: Eurex as of 14/02/2024, Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Bucket-wise, the Bobl net short positioning reached a new high over the last six months, and the Bund is close to its six-month max short. This fast accumulation of shorts at face value represents a risk for duration bears.

At this time we would be more cautious with interpreting the data. Discrepancies remain between price action on duration and the change in positioning (see Exhibit 35 ), hence we believe these shorts include a fair share of ASW trades, especially following the intrasession risk off episodes that led to short covering of ASW.

## Cash EGBs

In cash EGBs, we flag the richness of 10y Spain vs the 5y and 30y sector. Between the Spanish, Italian, and French curves, our EGBs 5s10s30s model points to an excessive richening of the 10y sector in the Spanish curve (see Exhibit 36 ).

Exhibit 36: Semi-core and non-core EGBs FV estimates.
<table><tr><td></td><td>BTPs 5s10s30s (6m changes)</td><td>SPGBs 5s10s30s (6m changes)</td><td>OATs 5s10s30s (6m changes)</td></tr><tr><td>Observed 5s10s30s (-ive = 10y richer)</td><td>-8.0</td><td>-10.7</td><td>-7.7</td></tr><tr><td>Fair value</td><td>-12.0</td><td>-1.1</td><td>-5.9</td></tr><tr><td>Gap to Fair Value (-ive = rich)</td><td>4.0</td><td>-9.6</td><td>-1.8</td></tr><tr><td>Z score (Gap to Fair Value)</td><td>0.4</td><td>-2.0</td><td>-0.5</td></tr><tr><td>Std Dev (bp)</td><td>9.6</td><td>4.8</td><td>3.8</td></tr><tr><td>R2</td><td>89%</td><td>88%</td><td>90%</td></tr></table>

Source: Morgan Stanley Research estimates

As a reminder, based on our proposed modelling (see here for the details), we discovered that most of the variance in 5s10s30s EGB flies can be explained as a function of (i) EUR 5s10s30s, (ii) 10y spread vs Bunds, (iii) medium term inflation expectations (i.e., EUR 5y5y or 10y BEI), and (iv) white/reds proxy. Across non-core and semi-core curves, these factors, on average, can explain around \~90% of the overall variance on 5s10s30s cash flies.

Exhibit 37: Residuals from Spain 5s10s30s Cash fly
![](images/e7fcb0beaecb79147c43f5961327cb23cb4a3329719a36af995e9965bbbe2e8a.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research estimates]

On constant maturity tickers, the model flags an excessive richness being worth \~10bp, equal to around \~2std deviations, amongst the biggest recorded historically (see Exhibit 37 ).

Additionally, we think that positioning on the country may be a bit crowded given the strong Spain’s fundamentals and investors’ preference to hold risk in Spain vs Italy or France. This has been reflected as well in the recent richening of 10y Spain vs France and Italy on credit barbells (see Exhibit 38 ).

Exhibit 38: 10y Italy/Spain/France credit barbel
![](images/0ab36a2f8e1b8c7d9faa7a76dc8933b40abde77e3d22ab30cd1b6dab19cb9dc6.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 39: 5s10s30s Spain vs 10y Bund
![](images/dddf854f112827b9380464d1309353545296d533fd558f7eecc96584980e6f2f.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Lastly, whilst the fly tended to be directional with duration, we have seen a marked decoupling of the 5s10s30s Spain vs the level of rates in February, which points to an excessive richening of the 10y point on the Spanish fly vis-à-vis the overall level of rates.

For the reasons listed, we enter a short SPGB 3.25% Apr 2034 vs SPGB 3.5% May 29 and SPGB 1.9% Oct 2052, currently at \~–20bp, targeting a move towards -15bp. Stop at -25bp.

The key risks to the trade include a continuation of the 10y spread compression in 10y Spain vs core or flows continuing to support the belly sector of the Spanish curve.

What about non-core spreads ?

Regarding non-core spreads, the marked tightening of BTP/Bund is in contrast to our previous expectations calling for a widening into 1Q as discussed in our 2024 Global Outlook. We were advocating for a widening in peripheral spreads based on (i) a deterioration of growth prospects in the euro area, (ii) the lack of fiscal consolidation keeping funding needs elevated, particularly for Italy, with the y/y difference in net supply increasing by \~€32bn and (iii) the threat of PEPP QT starting in 1Q, preventing particularly foreigners from re-entering into peripheral risk during the first half of the year in the context of no expected PEPP reinvestments during a busy 1Q issuance schedule.

Exhibit 40: Non-resident buying of BTPs by year
![](images/6661cbf71da89971da0ae5bd98aa8392804a9ec6237271028fb602038b5ed883.jpg)
Source: Bank of Italy, Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 41: Expect issuance of BTPs in DV01
![](images/4150ae6298295f2320fc8261c4c005a571159b767099bc16a0db341f027300e5.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research estimates

Nevertheless, since then, we had an improvement of economic data in Europe, as leading indicators like PMIs have been bottoming recently and are rebounding particularly in the periphery, coupled with the postponement (relative to our expectations) of PEPP QT to 2H 2024. All of this, as well as subdued political risk and the ongoing strong performance of both risky assets and credit, created a benign environment for BTPs outperformance, particularly on the premise that 2024 will see the materialization of ECB cuts.

Additionally, the recent Bank of Italy statistics have been pointing to non-resident investors piling into BTPs into the last quarter of 2023 (last available data point is as of Nov 2023). In 2023, total non-resident buying is totalling around EUR 47bn (see Exhibit 40 ) with roughly half of total 2023 buying occurring in the Sep/Nov 2023 period. Essentially since 2022, when BTPs saw non-residents selling EUR 44bn of Italian debt (the second biggest outflow recorded during a calendar year since 1990), non-resident exposure is flat.

As such, a continuation of the tightening in BTPs will likely be supported if the ongoing buying flows continue in 2024, which we find plausible as we factor in (i) the ample space to add into BTPs given the still-underweight positioning by the international community and (ii) the expected easing that the ECB will likely deliver, which in theory will be supportive for both risk and credit.

Exhibit 42: 10y BTP/Bund residuals vs Avg levels of Itraxx Main and X-Over
![](images/e21a03b98a2da434818bfa99072089d316e308046ea50265718745356855ae33.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research estimates

Additionally, although the 10y BTP/Bund is stuck at the tightest levels seen during the last few years, in the absence of a catalyst pushing the spread wider, a further tightening should not be excluded in the near term considering that (i) the 10y BTP/Bund remains \~8/9 cheap vs the average levels of credit indices, pointing to a fair value in the 10y spread around \~135.5bp and (ii) supply pressures are set to subside meaningfully over the next month (see Exhibit 41 ). All of this is happening in conjunction with the upcoming issuance of the BTP Valore (dedicated to retail investors only) from Feb 26th to March 1st.

This renewed source of demand was the main catalyst behind the tightening move in the 10y BTP/Bund spread back in June 2023, and on the premise that retail investors remain attracted to the overall level of yields, the issuance of the BTP Valore could again attract sizeable demand considering that the sell-off seen in the Euro-rates complex brought yields back to their pre December rally levels.

For the reason listed, we close our 10y BTP/Bund widener.

• Trade idea: Enter short SPGB 3.25% Apr 2034 vs SPGB 3.5% May 29 and SPGB 1.9% Oct 2052 currently at \~–20bp, targeting a move towards - 15bp. Stop at -25bp.

• Trade idea: Close 10y BTP/Bund widener

## ASW and SSA

Last week in Enter the Dragon, we entered a Bund/Buxl ASW box steepener (i.e., long Buxl ASW) trade on the premise of long-end supply meaningfully subsiding. Since then, the cheapening of ASW gained momentum, led by the Schatz, and boxes steepened.

The Bund/Buxl ASW box steepened 3bp+, which represents roughly a one-week standard deviation (over 3m computed with daily data). Long-end supply should only resume the first week of March for auctions; however, the AFT might also issue in the sector via syndication in two weeks. Hence, we close the trade.

## SSA Update

Looking at supply (see Exhibit 43 ), some issuers have already issued more than 30% of their estimated yearly target. The most significant progress can be attributed to CADES, with 2/3 of issuance in euro completed (relative to the mid-point of the estimated range). It is followed by EFSF with 55% and KFW (34%).

On the other hand, ESM and Unedic haven't come to the market yet. NRW issued only 11% of the yearly estimated volume, followed by EU, which completed 12% of yearly issuance (we estimate €130 to 150bn for this year). Rentenbank and Dutch agencies are slightly ahead with 15% to 19% completed.

Exhibit 43: Some issuers have already issued more than 30% of their estimated yearly target

As mentioned in Don't Call It a Comeback, our medium-term view is that of a cheapening of core ASW in 2024. SSA/core or SSA/semi-core spreads tend to be highly correlated to core ASW, as Exhibit 44 shows. These spreads are also positively correlated to the OAT/ Bund and BTP/Bund spreads (see Exhibit 45 with 10y EU/Bund), which explains the drop in correlation between SSA/core spreads and ASW in 4Q23. BTP/Bund spreads and ASW diverged in the BTP/Bund widening in September and October.

The correlation has now recovered with core ASW tightening alongside a richening of BTP/Bund spreads. In that context, SSAs could be an attractive instrument to play the medium-term view of a cheapening of core ASW, especially as semi-core and peripheral spreads don’t seem to have found a floor yet (see peripheral spreads section above).

Exhibit 44: SSA/core or SSA/semi-core spreads tend to be correlated to core ASW...
![](images/97c3d1f13a67baa6331f9f90f6b7abb9eeb0ead0cd31591cc8d664c3c237b5d4.jpg)

Exhibit 45: …and OAT/Bund and BTP/Bund spreads
![](images/c652923a89efe347415fefa619e3ef38d876d293667c5ea0de58c31104d51c44.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research; Note: 10y SSA yield is interpolated linearly
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research; Note: 10y SSA yield is interpolated linearly. For CADES, highest maturity bond is used

For investors who would wish to position for cheaper core ASW via long SSAs vs. Bunds or OATs, we think CADES and EFSF could be attractive from a supply perspective. They both have issued a large share of the estimate yearly target as mentioned above. Additionally, CADES could continue to trade rich relative to peers, in our opinion, given the social debt should be fully amortized by the end of 2033, meaning CADES will normally not be active in the aftermath.

Finally, last week in Enter the Dragon we argued short EU 34s on ASW presented a better risk/reward than short OAT or Bund ASW in case of a risk-off. This still holds, but the resumption of the BTP/Bund spread this week makes the trade less attractive, in our opinion; hence we close EU 34s ASW. Finally, we also close the EU 51s52s micro steepeners (opened in EU 2024 Issuance - A Few Factors Could Tip the Balance). The slope steepened back recently but less than expected as it failed to follow the steepening of OATs 30s50s.

• Trade idea: Close short Bund ASW vs Buxl ASW box

• Trade idea: Close sell EU 3.25% 2034 on ASW

• Trade idea: Close buy EU 0.7% 2051 versus EU 2.5% 2052

## Man Takeaways from Last week of Data

The past week has been particularly busy for the UK as plenty of data was released. Overall, data were mixed, and we think there are three main takeaways:

• The meaningful decrease in the unemployment rate to 3.8 %Y, the large swings in retail sales monthly figures (Retail Sales ex-fuels came at 3.4%M in January, versus -3.3%M in December, which was also revised lower), and backward revisions to both wage growth numbers and GDP figures call into question the reliability of the last data releases and their monetary policy implications

• Wage growth numbers surprised to the upside as private sector regular pay growth came at 6.2 %3M/Y while the BoE expected the print at 6.0% 3M/Y, in line with consensus. Although the surprise was partially driven by backwards revisions, we think the print adds hawkish risks to a near-term BoE dovish tilt. Having said that, the monthly readings still point to a clearly downward momentum (see Exhibit 48 )

• January CPI came in lower than consensus, and more importantly, the BoE's projections. The service reading came at 6.5%Y, significantly below consensus at 6.8%Y. While the extent of the downside surprise relates partially to re-weighting effects and the most volatile components (e.g., airfares), it stands out as a clear deceleration in service inflation from the most recent monthly prints in contrast to the euro area and the US (see Exhibit 49 ). This reinforce our view that the UK does not have any idiosyncratic inflation risk compared to other DM countries and makes cross-market valuations still attractive.

Exhibit 48: Private sector wage growth – a small uptick in monthly prints but a clearly downward trend
![](images/a0429447d13bc608fe33c41cbc198ed1fa2b4e0ee3ce96fcc702e3f52e921946.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 49: UK service inflation momentum points also to a downward trend in contrast to other DMs
![](images/8c828b582f1512ecc0794e4a33b4ba33d4b5373bb34eaec8da24f79cd89daa26.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

In term of market implications, we think the downside surprise on the inflation print increases the likelihood of monetary policy normalisation in the short term, and another soft inflation print in February could pave the way for a dovish pivot at the March MPC and a first cut as early as in May, which remains our modal view. Accordingly, we maintain our receiving May MPC position while noting that reds valuations turned particularly attractive to receive, with \~60bp of cuts being priced in 2H 2024 (see Exhibit 50 ). We think the sector offers compelling opportunity both via outright positions and MPC flatteners to also position for a delay in the easing cycle. In this regards, the main risks come from a hawkish Fed and the upcoming budget on March 6, 2024. On the former, a stronger than-expected economy and the risks for a re-acceleration in inflation could continue to cast doubt on how restrictive monetary policy is in the US, which might take out more curve inversion globally with the beta between UK and US rates recently having increased (see Exhibit 51 ). These considerations together with the improving UK inflation outlook makes cross-market trades attractive, in our view, and we continue to monitor valuations.

Exhibit 50: BoE easing expectations have been priced out with cumulative cuts in 2024 at \~70bp
![](images/b159e0e3fabf809fcf8af0e35c0513abb29207949e77110c7c3011ca704b7683.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 51: UK vs US rates beta is rising, with US data driving most of the price action
![](images/1a0ad48e99724ffd00a1c0af17128d392d62640ea346d68d158b44faa36bd294.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

However, the budget also remains a risk to monetary policy normalisation, especially in the short term. Recent headlines suggested less fiscal headroom than previously expected after the repricing in the BoE's lower easing expectations and the last GDP figures, which are slightly above the November 2023 projections, reducing the likelihood for large giveaways. We still think that meaningful under-spending is also unlikely given electoral incentives – hence, short-term supply implications should remain fairly in line with our forecasts, as we expect an increase in gilt supply compared to the current year and a bigger contribution for mediums.

## Upcoming Supply – Ultras and Linkers Considerations

Next week will be light on the data-front, with February PMIs the most relevant release. We think the market will mostly focus on supply given that the reopening of the 4 63s tap is expected on Tuesday (£1.75bn nominal and \~£3m/bp DV01), likely the last ultras supply for the current quarter both in nominal and linkers space. The bonds have recently cheapened on RV, and yields valuations look slightly attractive (see Exhibit 52 ). On RV, the bond should richen from current valuations given limited supply ahead, but weakerthan-expected auctions pose risks to this view.

Exhibit 52: 4 63s RV valuations have cheapened recently, ahead of the upcoming supply
![](images/370df20257e2c52f515db64c4898102817cf0512978ebad7ca25b8a13c4b8393.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Exhibit 53: UK real yields' recent flattening could be short-lived given the upcoming syndication
![](images/ff334d555813fad641d94cf0860f763bf54458ac3ddc7042c56fdc30eb92546e.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

The other important supply event will be the linker syndication, which is expected in March. Given the budget is scheduled for March 6, 2024, the week starting in March 11 seems the most plausible choice. The maturity of the bond has not been yet decided, and the new 30y linker seems the most likely one (54s maturity), while the reopening of the 45s line also possible. It is worth noting that the syndication will bring a significant amount of DV01; a new 30y linker should bring \~£10 m/bp given \~£3.8bn estimated cash size (\~£3bn remaining for linker syndications and \~£800m of the unallocated pot).

In terms of markets implications, first, the linker syndications can impact long-end nominal valuations given the two markets are interconnected, which challenges the view that after the upcoming 63s auction there is no more supply in longs in the current FY. Second, in linkers space, the recent flattening of the curve in RY and even more pronounced in ASW, as well as the upcoming supply, might add re-steepening pressures. In addition, crossmarket linkers valuations have richened significantly versus TIPS, which reduces overseas demand in particular (see Exhibit 54 and Exhibit 56 ).

The richening in cross-market valuations is also notable in nominal space. We think this could limit any further richening in long-end gilts ASWs and makes the 10s30s ASW box particularly attractive given 1) the low-supply story for the remainder of the FY is likely priced in, 2) cross-market valuations have richened, which reduces demand from overseas and could trigger opposite cross-market flows (e.g., linkers into TIPS), 3) BPA flows are still lingering, and the recent richening in long-end ASWs has improved market conditions, and 4) mortality assumption changes will remain a relevant topic in the LDI space and might warrant an ongoing reduction in LDI accounts liabilities.

That said, credit spreads remain historically tight and at unfavorable levels for switching gilts into credit, which might limit any buyout-related flows (see Exhibit 55 ). We thus continue to monitor any widening in credit spreads, which could ultimately trigger BPArelated flows and cheapen the 30y ASW on the 10s30s box.

Exhibit 54: Gilts and linkers ASW cross-market valuations have richened significantly, which reduces their attractiveness
![](images/052723ade195a0cdff17fa4a80a5baa9599b53f5112e8d5d67ad07c377e9c7c5.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 55: Richer ASW valuations make buyout flows more attractive for insurers – however, credit spreads remain at historical tight levels
![](images/af25cf212d288a2e8cbc5482e84d2ba073f2bd11cc3777a5ebe65220a7efafe0.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

# Japan | Searching for the appropriate normalization path

MORGAN STANLEY MUFG SECURITIES CO., LTD.

## Koichi Sugisaki

Koichi.Sugisaki@morganstanleymufg.com

+81 3 6836-8428

Belly-zone JGBs faced renewed correction pressure in the wake of this week’s strongerthan-expected US CPI print, but regained lost ground after domestic GDP data for 4Q 2023 showed a second straight contraction, and US retail sales data then surprised very much on the downside.

The market-implied BoJ rate hike trajectory over the next year or so has however been little changed even as these various indicators continue to command so much attention (see Exhibit 57 , Exhibit 58 ), with the consensus still pointing to an exit from the negative interest rate policy (NIRP) in either March or April, as would be consistent with recent hints from Governor Kazuo Ueda and Deputy Governor Shinichi Uchida.

Exhibit 57: Market implied BoJ policy path around 4Q GDP preliminary release
![](images/3cc411e1360543162eb81ed489146b73a1955d0107d059ae1dff696b5be2eff3.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Exhibit 58: Market implied pace of hikes in the following 12 months
![](images/bc2e76160d24fc517d2e80d4ac45e80ec5bd5337f07af12cfda5a4ecb155d115.jpg)
Morgan Stanley Japan Pace of 25bp Hikes in Following 12 Months Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

The focus of market attention meanwhile appears to have shifted to the (as yet highly uncertain) longer-term rate hike trajectory, with the BoJ having effectively signaled that it will depend on exogenous circumstances as well as what data at any given point in time are saying about economic, inflation, and financial conditions.

This week’s services inflation data have intensified concerns that US inflation might prove stickier than envisaged as the economy continues to perform so solidly.

With an upward repricing of short-term US r\* having driven USD/JPY beyond 150, our impression is that rises in medium-term JGB yields in the first half of the week reflected somewhat of a hawkish rethink as market participants considered both (1) the possibility of the BoJ hiking the rates at a rapid pace with a view to curbing the JPY’s slide (see Exhibit 59 ) and (2) the potential for domestic inflation expectations to be driven higher by a renewed acceleration in import costs ( Exhibit 60 ).

Real GDPNon-residential Investment Household Consumption

Exhibit 59: Market implied pace of hikes in the following 12months after fist hike, and USD/JPY
![](images/fe6f0887af8eaec50e514d06a169ccc8740d6858985f2b5d71cc8465710dc530.jpg)
Market Implied pace of 25bp hikes after 1st hike (avg 5 days)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Exhibit 60: USD/JPY and Survey based inflation
![](images/4dc1a9e17efe225924c71d848671142c683cacffc0d720eb497aafa1411d2193.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Quick

But the “stronger growth equals stickier inflation” thesis then took a hit after the latest US retail sales data proved so unexpectedly weak, and figures for previous months were also revised downwards. That has left some market participants concluding that the January CPI print may ultimately have been an outlier attributable to residual seasonality.

Japan GDP data for 4Q 2023 showed a second successive contraction (–0.1% QoQ, see Exhibit 61 ). Domestic demand components were basically weak across the board, with private consumption (household spending) and private nonresidential investment (corporate capex) each shrinking for a third straight quarter. Such figures were of course consistent with the observed weakness of the BoJ’s real consumption activity index for 4Q (see Exhibit 62 ).

Exhibit 61: Real GDP QoQ, SA, household
consumption, and non-residential investment
![](images/df0d6aadfe1b74f9ade6ffb4b689f0883eebf46929b9eba21bb3eb382f4fec21.jpg)
Source: Cabinet office, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 62: Real consumption activity index
![](images/2e7d859a16bd28fa984e0b2e71f561ca830363e770b847f546a3e23a6a3b77e6.jpg)
Source: BoJ, Morgan Stanley Research

Our conversations with investors suggest that many are concerned that growth could come in negative once again for 1Q 2024 due to the impact of the New Year’s Day Noto Peninsula earthquake, as well as the suspension of some production by a major automaker, and that this might in turn end up derailing the BoJ’s plans for monetary policy “normalization”.

In a speech made the day after the release of the BoJ’s real consumption activity index for 4Q, Deputy Governor Uchida signaled that the central bank will be paying particular attention to household spending trends as it seeks confirmation of higher wages being reflected in services prices:

(…) there is no event that can be an indicator comparable to the annual spring labor-management wage negotiations. However, the Bank will make a comprehensive judgment based on factors such as price developments, mainly for services prices, and developments in private consumption that support them.

Given this focus on how private consumption is tracking, our economists see a risk of the weak 4Q GDP print leading the BoJ to hold off on a NIRP exit until April, with a view to collecting more data on how services prices are being affected by faster wage growth.

## Suggested positioning

Markets continue to recalibrate their expectations for the BoJ’s medium- to long-term policy rate trajectory in reaction to exogenous developments and new data. That said, our overall impression is that it is becoming increasingly difficult to price in a much faster pace of rate hikes given that US interest rates now appear to have stopped climbing and domestic economic indicators are meanwhile far from strong.

With the belly of the curve increasingly volatile depending on incoming data/the external environment, however, we basically prefer any bullish duration exposure on the wings rather than on the belly of the curve.

We continue to view the short end as offering an attractive risk/reward balance, with carry + rolldown still eminently appealing and expectations for BoJ rate hikes looking unlikely to be dramatically ramped up. As such we suggest sticking with 2y outright receiver positions.

We also continue to see value in body-long 10s20s30s butterflies, with the 20y sector looking cheaper than appears consistent with UST yield levels and the market-implied BoJ rate hike trajectory (see Exhibit 63 ) and thus seemingly standing a good chance of outperforming if UST yield start to head lower (see Exhibit 64 ).

We basically feel comfortable maintaining a duration-long bias via this butterfly structure given that UST yields now appear to be settling down and domestic catalysts for shorting duration are by no means obvious.

Moreover, we are sticking with our 30y JGB ASW short recommendation as a means of focusing on supply/demand headwinds in the 30y JGB sector. With life insurers seemingly reluctant to step up their purchases until NIRP is actually ended, we basically see ample • Trade idea: Maintain receive 2y OIS outright

scope for 30y JGBs to underperform 30y OIS for lack of demand.
Exhibit 63: Fair value implied in 10y UST yield and market implied pace of hikes in the following 12 months

# Currency & Foreign Exchange

## United States

## USD | Defying the norm

The magnitude of the dollar's reaction to the January US CPI data was much larger than historical evidence on the USD's response to upside surprises in inflation data suggests. Based on intra-day trading data covering around 200 US CPI releases for which the median Bloomberg estimate is available, there are only a handful of instances of larger intra-day USD increases to higher-than expected CPI data than on February 13. We think that the strong co-movement between the US dollar and the market-implied pace of Fed cuts since late 2022 explains the uncommon size of the USD response.

We remain neutral on the US dollar. Continued US growth outperformance, a potentially stronger tightening bias from the Fed, and risks around a hard landing should all be USD-positive. On the other hand, some signs of growth expectations rebounding from low levels in Europe and resilient US stock markets may be USD headwinds.

While remaining neutral, we retain our USD bullish skew due to the sizeable above consensus forecast for US GDP from our economics team and risks of the "inflation reacceleration" narrative gaining traction in FX markets.

## Europe

## EUR/USD | The hedge of reason

YTD markets have traded very much in regime 2 of our four-regime FX framework – higher real rates and wider breakevens. Among other dynamics, this has pushed FX vol lower and the USD-equity correlation to near zero. However, this is unlikely to last, and there is a window for investors to add cheap USD-long hedges.

EUR/USD puts are an attractive hedge for a variety of macro scenarios, in our view. A re-acceleration in US inflation (regime 3) or hard landing concerns (regime 4) both see a stronger USD and higher FX volatility. Even staying in regime 2 marginally weighs on EUR/USD despite higher equities.

Regime 2 is unique in seeing the USD-equity correlation turn flat. A shift to any other regime historically sees this correlation turn more negative. Thus the equity declines seen in regimes 3 and 4 would likely be offset by gains on the EUR/USD put from both spot and vol, while regime 1 likely sees equities gaining in excess of the lost EUR/USD put premium.

## CHF | Cut in the ski season?

Probably not. Despite January inflation data largely confirming the disinflation narrative taking place in Switzerland, cutting policy in March may be premature.

One can argue that the SNB has the inflation "space" to cut policy in March, given the progress already made. However, the SNB defines price stability as ranging from 0% to 2% inflation. Hence, given current levels of inflation, there is also an argument to be made that the SNB can remain restrictive for longer before having to deal with deflation.

While the SNB does need to take growth into account in its quest for price stability, economic activity while weak, does not yet seem to require intervention. Slightly stronger growth may be desirable, but not at the risk of re-accelerating inflation. Hence, June may ultimately be the more likely contender for a first cut.

## Dollar Bloc

We recommend long AUD/NZD positions. Our high-frequency fair value model suggests that AUD/NZD has risen less than highly correlated assets would have predicted. We see room for RBA policy to nudge Australian front end yields higher. AUD/NZD has continued to trade below where yield differentials would suggest.

And we see room for front-end New Zealand rates to move lower. While employment and non-tradeable CPI data have been more resilient than the RBNZ expected, GDP and headline inflation were slightly soft and declining inflation expectations have declined. Therefore we do not expect the RBNZ to meaningfully shift its OCR forecast higher, likely reducing front-end New Zealand yields.

## Japan

## JPY | Is there a risk of potential MoF intervention?

We discuss the potential risk of MoF intervention now that USD/JPY has moved above the 150.00 level once again, primarily driven by a surprisingly stronger US CPI print. MoF officials have already sent a signal that the recent rapid FX move is speculative, and they will closely monitor FX levels with a high sense of urgency.

However, in our view, the MoF is only likely to be concerned by the speed at which the JPY has been sliding, with currency weakness itself probably not considered all that problematic. This is because import prices have already declined from the recent peak even in a JPY denominated basis, and CPI inflation has already decelerated towards the BoJ's 2% goal. We believe that any eventual intervention decision is likely to be made in response to JPY depreciation that is deemed “excessive”. We maintain short EUR/JPY, given that the risk of "hard landing" events currently appears to be greatest in Europe, where the economy continues to struggle.

The magnitude of the dollar's reaction to the January US CPI data was much larger than historical evidence on the USD's response to upside surprises in inflation data suggests. We remain neutral on the US dollar for now as G10 currencies are still trading in Regime 2, characterized by mixed dollar performance.

As one would expect, the dollar tends to strengthen following upside surprises in US CPI data when inflation is above target, as higher than expected price pressures may have an impact on policy expectations and interest rate differentials ( Exhibit 66 ).

This week's CPI report was accompanied by a sharp rise in the US dollar. Comparing the standardized CPI surprises since 2007 to intra-day movements of the DXY index shows that the increase in the USD was larger than usual.

Not only did the DXY rise more than historically in the immediate aftermath of the print ( Exhibit 67 ), but also by 4pm NY time was the USD strength more pronounced than on other trading days included in our sample ( Exhibit 68 ).

Based on data covering around 200 US CPI releases for which the median Bloomberg estimate is available, there are only two instances where the USD saw a sharper increase between 8am and 10am NY time. Expanding the time window to 4pm to take into consideration some of the retracement still shows only eight trading days on which the USD strengthened more from upside surprises to inflation than on February 13.

Exhibit 68: The DXY increase between 8am-4pm NY time after the January CPI release was much larger than historical reactions to upside CPI surprises

We think that the strong co-movement between the US dollar and the market-implied pace of Fed cuts (MSP1UT Index) since late 2022 may explain the uncommon response of the US dollar to the January CPI report ( Exhibit 69 ).

On February 13, the MSP1UT Index saw one of the largest daily declines since our rates colleagues started tracking this index ( Exhibit 70 ), implying expectations of less interest rate cuts from the Federal Reserve.

Exhibit 70: The MSP1UT Index saw one of the largest daily declines after the upside surprise in the US January CPI...
![](images/c7bd4470cb45138186b173c25a36e992c555eb2154ce53294af63761e166ffc5.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 71: ...but the index remains within a range observed in 2H23
![](images/f9b5bf215988994c67b30f930c26b0cfaecb1fb9cda87f44bd0e8ba2481775a3.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

But did the abnormal USD reaction constitute a turning point for the G10 FX narrative? We think it is too soon to tell, as there were some unusual underlying details in the CPI report (see The Devil You Know Is Better Than The Data You Don't ). We recommend EUR/USD puts for a transition into another regime with a clearer dollar direction (see EUR/USD | The hedge of reason ).

The main factors for the USD to rise over the next months are (1) continued US growth outperformance combined with a stronger tightening bias in Fed communication, (2) markets pricing in a greater probability of a reaccerleration in inflation, and (3) a hard landing.

Our economists' US growth forecast remains above consensus, even as the median Bloomberg estimate has increased towards the projections of our economists since last fall ( Exhibit 72 ). US data outperforming the rest of the world, with additional evidence that the January CPI report was not an outlier would likely be USD-positive. However, based on our analysis on the USD-reaction to the January inflation data it is unlikely that intraday DXY moves from a US CPI print will continue to be as large as on February 13.

While not our economists' base case, a global hard landing can also not be ruled out given the restrictiveness of monetary policy - an outcome which should see safe haven flows into the USD ( Exhibit 73 ).

On the other hand, the main factors that might lead to USD declines over the next months are (1) a rebound in global growth expectations, and (2) further resilience of equity markets.

Growth in economies outside the US has remained slow over the past quarters. However, there are some signs that point to European growth expectations into 2H24 improving ( Exhibit 74 ). Meanwhile our China economists highlight the April or July Politburo meetings as potential catalysts for meaningful fiscal stimulus.
