US stock markets remaining resilient could be another headwind for the USD, or limit the extent to which the USD may rise. Historically, the performance of the S&P 500 into the first rate cut from the Fed has been mixed ( Exhibit 75 ), even during years that have been defined as soft-landings (see Blinder (2023) Landings, Soft and Hard: The Federal Reserve, 1965–2022). Risk demand during this cutting cycle will be the key factor to determine whether we enter Regime 1, i.e., the most USD negative outcome.

While remaining neutral, we retain our USD bullish skew due to the sizeable above consensus forecast for US GDP from our economics team and risks of the "inflation reacceleration" narrative gaining traction in FX markets.

## EUR/USD | The hedge of reason

<table><tr><td rowspan="5">MORGAN STANLEY &amp; CO. INTERNATIONAL PLC</td><td>David S. Adams, CFA</td></tr><tr><td>David.S.Adams@morganstanley.com</td></tr><tr><td>Wanting Low</td></tr><tr><td>Wanting.Low@morganstanley.com +44 20 7425-6841 Dominic Krummenacher</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="2">+44 20 7425-9781</td></tr><tr><td>Dominic.Krummenacher@morganstanley.com</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="3">MORGAN STANLEY &amp; CO. LLC</td><td>Andrew Watrous</td></tr><tr><td>Andrew.Watrous@morganstanley.com</td></tr><tr><td>Zoe Strauss</td></tr><tr><td>Zoe.Strauss@morganstanley.com</td><td>+1 212 761-0407</td></tr></table>

Price action YTD has largely corresponded with regime 2 of our four-regime USD framework. US real rates and breakevens have moved higher, generating both a stronger USD and higher equities (see Exhibit 76 ).

Exhibit 76: YTD price action has been firmly in regime 2 of our four-regime USD framework
![](images/38fe94d9791d2214c4cc232f6d74e2e9dc541e89147e827cc03b3e0fae2ed7cd.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 77: Cross-asset price action has largely conformed to historical regime 2 patterns...

![](images/842d99faec67eabe0e36dd688a3b1b448c5fb1db08a6e202cc0f5165aeb1919b.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Even 'under the hood' we see regime 2 playing out within asset classes. In commodities, oil is outperforming industrial commodities, in turn beating out gold, which has declined as expected. Internal equity market dynamics are also consistent with what regime 2 implies (see Exhibit 77 ).

G10 FX is also trading relatively consistently with what regime 2 predicts but with a generally USD-higher bias (see Exhibit 78 ). That is, the relative ordering of performance versus USD is consistent; the magnitudes of USD gains are far higher.

Regime 2 is a tricky one to trade for currencies because the two key forces driving USD – risk appetite and real interest rates – are pulling in opposite directions. Equities are rising (USD down) while real rates are as well (USD up). Historically speaking, USD's strength is more evenly balanced by these forces, explaining the average annualized return of just about 2.5% of the DXY (20bp/month).

USD's outperformance versus historical forces reflects the 'what to buy' problem investors are facing. When we talk to investors, we sense reluctance to buy major currencies given growth weakness and relatively lower yields. This is partly why positioning has turned so long USD, particularly in the options space (see Exhibit 79 ).

Exhibit 78: ...though USD has gained more than predicted...
![](images/afb52c89ce1e1d8fa9eaf04f8dbec0218cd77dfda71d747465588512dc8e2802.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

![](images/ef1d6a7adade45ed92e92ebd876908c77502663a6c29a5284fee14e4a333f910.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

The European outlook remains challenged, amid low growth, rapid disinflation, and clear headwinds from China. Central banks in Europe including the ECB but also perhaps the BoE and Riksbank could cut faster and deeper than the Fed.

Concern about China output has left investors, based on our conversations, reluctant to deploy capital in the region. Of the few currencies that investors generally do want to buy (which are typically in the higher carry EM space), positioning is already seen as crowded and the trades in line with consensus.

Incoming US data such as January employment and, most recently, US CPI have further concretized the view that the Fed, even if cutting this year, is likely to be more gradual and cautious compared to peers.

An interesting consequence of these dynamics is that changes in USD have become increasingly decorrelated to changes in equities (see Exhibit 80 ). This is reasonably evident for the DXY but far more clear with some other currencies (see Exhibit 81 ).

We think this uncorrelated dynamic is both temporary and advantageous: it suggests that long USD could be an important – and positive-carrying – hedge for long equity portfolios. We recommend that investors buy 1y EUR/USD puts struck near current spot levels (1.07) as a result.

Exhibit 80: USD's correlation to equities has turned flat.
![](images/68cdda9a8a007444dd4ef6eb76aec8522c8c54d04fc9894771a34db3dbc05a47.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 81: ...though this may prove a temporary phenomenon, offering a cheap window for equity hedges
![](images/1c53c1321eb401ae983ccbb1f5de03cefae5d6f2c3d937d32b9d5c0e2563dd97.jpg)

The hedge of reason covers many seasons: We think this hedge is attractive in large part because of its versatility – it should cover the investor in a variety of different scenarios. We summarize how we expect equities, EUR/USD, and the components of the EUR/USD 1y put to perform in each of the regimes in Exhibit 82 .

First, let's say we stay in regime 2 for a while. Historically speaking, equities do quite well in this scenario (the best of the four regimes) while EUR/USD falls about 6bp per month – roughly unchanged but with a bit of additional benefit.

Exhibit 82: How do EUR/USD and EUR/USD
optionality perform in different regime scenarios?
<table><tr><td></td><td>Regime 1</td><td>Regime 2</td><td>Regime 3</td><td>Regime 4</td></tr><tr><td>10y Real Yield</td><td>↓</td><td>↑</td><td>↑</td><td>↓</td></tr><tr><td>10y Breakeven</td><td>↑</td><td>↑</td><td>↓</td><td>↓</td></tr><tr><td>Average Monthly Return (bp)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>S&amp;P 500</td><td>191</td><td>242</td><td>-59</td><td>-116</td></tr><tr><td>USD/EUR</td><td>-127</td><td>6</td><td>78</td><td>23</td></tr><tr><td>FXVIX</td><td>-26</td><td>-40</td><td>30</td><td>30</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3">EUR/USD ATMS 1y Put Return Contributions</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Spot</td><td></td><td>+</td><td>+</td><td>+</td></tr><tr><td>Forward Points</td><td>+</td><td>一</td><td>一</td><td>+</td></tr><tr><td>FX Volatility</td><td>_</td><td></td><td>+</td><td>+</td></tr></table>

Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research; Note: For forward points foreign yields are assumed to be unchanged versus falling or rising US yields.

Exhibit 83: Implied EUR/USD volatility has fallen, rendering options cheaper to buy
![](images/34483a2022604e32b8c10497d568500caa4837538dcbd65b0775c2a3498e27db.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Rising US rates push the forward points steeper while vol falls, amplifying the lost premium on the option, but this is partly offset by the move in spot and likely more than offset by the gain in equities given that equities tend to rise the most in regime 2 (nearly 2.5% per month on average).

What if US inflation re-accelerates, taking Fed rate cuts off the table, adding some probability of additional hikes, and pushing us into regime 3? This is the best scenario for USD in spot. Higher US rates steepen the forward points, lowering delta, but the rise in vol coupled with the spot move likely more than offsets this. The equity underperformance should be reasonably well hedged by the gains on the USD leg.

However, regime 4 would be the best performance for the USD hedge – and the worst outcome for the equity leg. Falling rates and a flatter forward curve coupled with a higher USD in spot and higher FX vol all support the value of the EUR/USD put. This scenario most likely corresponds with a US and global hard landing.

On its own, regime 1 would be the biggest risk to being short EUR/USD. It's by far the most USD-negative scenario and tends to suppress FX vol. Granted, falling US rates offset the lost premium from spot and vol moves, but overall it's not good for the trade. Of course, a long equity position also hedges against this risk on the other side.

It's worth mentioning that even if we do enter regime 1, we think the risk is that USD's weakness is smaller than history suggests. This is because investors are still generally skeptical about the RoW growth outlook and increasingly focused on US election risks.

It's also worth remembering that the correlation of USD to equities in each of the nonregime 2 regimes is more negative. It's only in regime 2 that we see this correlation turn closer to zero. This suggests that investor interest in equity-USD correlation swaps could rise if they begin to think we are entering a new regime.

Why a 1y option? We think a 1y option is attractive for two key reasons. First, EUR/USD implied vols are cheap (see Exhibit 83 ). Relative to history, implied volatility is near a two-year low, while risk reversals suggest that the cost of purchasing downside protection has fallen relative to upside. Thus, in addition to the benefits from the cross-cutting forces of spot, vol, and forward points, optionality is increasingly a cheap method of expressing downside views on EUR/USD.

Second, a 1y option is attractive to investors in part because it covers the election window (even though it includes greater time decay). Material changes in US foreign and trade policy are likely to be USD positive, particularly versus EUR, where investors, based on our conversations, appear increasingly concerned about the impact of policy changes. We think this risk amplifies the attractiveness of USD and EUR/USD downside optionality in the eyes of investors. This is also why we prefer EUR/USD versus other crosses.

The key risk to the trade is that US real interest rates fall for a benign reason. This would push real rates lower, equities higher, and likely lead to widespread USD weakness. This is consistent with regime 1 of our four-regime framework.

• Trade idea: Buy EUR/USD 1y put near at-the-money-spot (ATMS, 1.07) for 1.76%

## NZD | Long AUD/NZD into the RBNZ

<table><tr><td rowspan="2">MORGAN STANLEY &amp; CO. LLC</td><td>Andrew Watrous</td><td>+1 212 761-5287</td></tr><tr><td>Andrew.Watrous@morganstanley.com</td><td></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="6">MORGAN STANLEY &amp; CO. INTERNATIONAL PLC</td><td>David S. Adams, CFA</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>David.S.Adams@morganstanley.com</td><td>+44 20 7425-3518</td></tr><tr><td>Wanting Low</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Wanting.Low@morganstanley.com</td><td>+44 20 7425-6841</td></tr><tr><td>Dominic Krummenacher</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Dominic.Krummenacher@morganstanley.com</td><td>+44 20 7425-9781</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="2">MORGAN STANLEY &amp; CO. LLC</td><td>Zoe Strauss</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Zoe.Strauss@morganstanley.com</td><td>+1 212 761-0407</td></tr></table>

We recommend long AUD/NZD positions into the February RBNZ meeting for several reasons:

First, our high-frequency fair value model suggests that AUD/NZD has risen less than highly correlated assets would have predicted. Movements in short-end yield differentials between Australia and New Zealand and China growth-sensitive stock prices would have suggested that AUD/NZD should be roughly 1.1% above current levels.

Second, we see room for RBA policy to nudge Australian front-end yields higher – relative to New Zealand yields but also more broadly. Current market pricing is consistent with nearly two 25bp cuts from the RBA.

However, the RBA sees the costs of resiliently high growth and inflation as larger than the costs of a sharper-than-expected growth slowdown. The RBA has also stated its concern about resiliently high services inflation, and has noted that its policy stance is not as restrictive as that of other central banks. We see these considerations justifying the Board keeping rates on hold through year end, likely boosting front-end Australian rates.

Exhibit 84: AUD/NZD trades below yield differentials...
![](images/9c55c55814941489773185c8610bcd5d0e85b3ba64ae9dd1f7bcaddc6bc48b3a.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

![](images/878399a105127c0d83ef320cea8bd6a9d0c79ec10e6fbd6c1d36edfc237764ed.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Third, AUD/NZD has continued to trade below where yield differentials would suggest (as Exhibit 84 illustrates). We see these yield differentials as supportive for the pair over time.

Fourth, we see room for front-end New Zealand rates to move lower. On a relatively short time horizon, the trajectory of AUD/NZD has reflected relative RBNZ and RBA policy expectations ( Exhibit 85 ). In turn, the differential in near term RBA and RBNZ policy expectations has been highly sensitive to recent New Zealand data prints.

RBNZ expectations declined sharply following a soft 3Q GDP print ( Exhibit 86 ). At the November 28 RBNZ forecast meeting, the bank said it expected 3Q GDP would rise at a 0.3% Q/Q SAAR rate. Instead, data released two weeks later showed GDP contracted by 0.3% – and 2Q GDP was downwardly revised by 0.4 percentage points.

Exhibit 86: NZ Z4 pricing has been highly responsive to recent data
![](images/e03d2a3a177069055592cc0660079d73ff903faa245142d07d49174c6674fb47.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 87: The 3m bank bill rate has risen in line with OIS
![](images/cc342a65098c0ee0e56b563a3cca7f1ae307530ea14566f1a9583ae6cf74aa12.jpg)

However, since the 3Q GDP print, rate expectations for New Zealand have risen sharply – particularly over the past two weeks ( Exhibit 87 ). Multiple factors contributed to that rise, including:

A) 4Q non-tradeable inflation was somewhat faster than the bank had forecast ( Exhibit 96 ). That rise in non-tradeable inflation raises the likelihood that the RBNZ lifts its forecast for non-tradeable inflation through the forecast horizon. A stronger-thanexpected 4Q inflation print raised the possibility that the RBNZ would conclude that nontradeable inflation would return to the top of its inflation target range within the forecast horizon.

While overall prices rose somewhat slower in 4Q than the RBNZ had forecast ( Exhibit 89 ), the downside surprise was driven primarily by tradeables inflation ( Exhibit 90 ). Governor Orr recently downplayed the importance of the decline in tradeable inflation, noting that "movements in tradable inflation are also strongly influenced by temporary shocks."

Exhibit 88: Non-tradeable inflation has been slightly more stubborn than the RBNZ forecast...
![](images/df9d8b14002e666e32e6fb23d8b1c4ca24360e9712cb51fe0ac6a3fcf6b2ccd8.jpg)
Source: RBNZ, Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 89: ...but headline surprised the RBNZ to the downside
![](images/78a2262302004db3940ef6b70953c25a31f529c5af283bafb6bb8681b350fe9c.jpg)
Source: RBNZ, Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 91 shows the RBNZ's three most recent OCR forecasts. The RBNZ's OCR forecast is the path that "ensure[s] that, absent of any further shocks, inflation still returns to the 2 percent midpoint over the medium term." The OCR forecast already implied that further hikes would be necessary to ensure that inflation returned to 2%, and the non-tradeable inflation upside surprise raised the risk that these hikes might materialize.

Exhibit 90: Tradeable inflation has decelerated quickly
![](images/221fe809656a6c6f7f92f770b22bfff353e9b463e665f4822fe3226476e65d8f.jpg)
Source: RBNZ, Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 91: We do not expect a substantial further upward revision to the OCR forecast
![](images/4d3a7a289b03fd1e4555f969e2fcd1d5ad37b410dd79eba81785432994362b4c.jpg)
Source: RBNZ, Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

B) RBNZ Chief Economist speech. On January 30 RBNZ Chief Economist Paul Conway signaled that data received since the November meeting did not clearly provide reassurance that inflation pressures would abate.

Conway noted that "recent [downside] GDP revisions do not necessarily mean that capacity pressures in the economy are much lower than previously assumed" due to the fact that consumption and business investment were almost exactly as the RBNZ had forecast in November.

Conway concluded that "monetary policy is working, with the economy slowing and inflation falling. But we still have a way to go to get inflation back to the target midpoint."

As discussed above, in recent weeks these factors have sharply raised the market-implied path of near-term RBNZ policy. A significant chance of an RBNZ hike by this summer has now been priced, and at least one local bank has predicted the RBNZ will raise rates further.

However, we do not see significant further room for markets to price an additional further likelihood of RBNZ hikes from the February meeting.

The RBNZ warned in November that "if inflationary pressures were to be stronger than anticipated, the OCR would likely need to increase further." In our view, data released since November do not likely meet the RBNZ's bar of representing "stronger-thananticipated inflationary pressures."

Exhibit 92: Core inflation fell sharply...
![](images/0965b10489b65da83534138db28fad203fc13035d76ea84cd127e83864647370.jpg)
Source: RBNZ, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 93: ...and inflation expectations have declined further
![](images/80f463253661c754b9e99fdd3c7011a08726ff5fd78cda43cc9770c87e7f2a2a.jpg)

4Q core inflation decelerated meaningfully on a y/y basis. The RBNZ's factor model (released on January 24) dropped a full percentage point, to below 4% ( Exhibit 92 ). And 1Q inflation expectations (released on February 12) fell meaningfully toward the 2% inflation target midpoint ( Exhibit 93 ).

And the drop in headline 4Q inflation is likely to further assuage concerns about elevated inflation expectations – even if it was mostly driven by softer-than-expected tradeables prices.

We think it is unlikely that the RBNZ will publish a significantly higher OCR forecast, given downside surprises in GDP and headline CPI since the November meeting. As a result, we expect front-end yields to decline as investors reduce the likelihood of further hikes.

• Trade idea: Enter Long AUD/NZD at 1.0659 with a target of 1.12 and stop of 1.04

• Trade idea: Maintain receive New Zealand 2y OIS at 5.06

• Trade idea: Maintain pay New Zealand 10y OIS at 4.57

• Trade idea: Maintain pay Australia 2y OIS at 4.03

• Trade idea: Maintain receive Australia 10y OIS at 4.25

## CHF | Cut in the ski season?

Probably not - for now.

Despite inflation data in Switzerland moderating, the width of the SNB's target band, combined with an economic situation that appears soft but not markedly weak, likely shifts probabilities of the first cut to June rather than March.

January's CPI print was soft with headline, core (ex fresh & seasonal products, energy, and fuels), and supercore (core ex administrated prices) continuing their downward trajectory ( Exhibit 94 ).

Even when breaking down the CPI basket, the data is encouraging. The cost of domestic goods and services, which thus far has proven to be stubborn, continued to disinflate at an annual level, once again reaching 2%Y ( Exhibit 95 ).

Exhibit 94: Headline, core, and supercore CPI baskets continue to moderate and exhibit downside momentum
![](images/4098f2314b67ecacb1b6bbbf077c7ed5b4f89446c309fca7ce7a14536ee3d014.jpg)
Source: Federal Statistical Office of Switzerland (FSO), Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 95: Inflation in domestic goods and services has finally slowed to 2%Y once again

![](images/e8e43832bc02daa4c174b2c3e22bc42ba816ae43ffa39c4726afa9ae6ffa52b7.jpg)
Source: FSO, Morgan Stanley Research

On a monthly basis, the domestic basket seems optically strong with +0.6%M, but can largely be explained away by seasonals. Over the past 10 years, the price of domestic goods and services has relatively consistently risen more in January than the rest of the calendar year ( Exhibit 96 ).

This unfortunately does also negate a large part of the deflation seen in the import basket. However, not sufficiently to truly challenge the disinflation narrative.

Exhibit 96: On average, in the last 10 years January has exhibited strength in the domestic basket and weakness in the import basket
![](images/3266e858c494443ac22d7999b936ff47a1b28a17f693ffe1338b6c705d573527.jpg)
Source: FSO, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 97: Services inflation is proving to be sticky, as it continues to trend higher
![](images/a099c7ea5e9c6d2217811063622c299355b544a802ebae2d7b78fd6e01f760d8.jpg)
Source: FSO Morgan Stanley Research

The hold-out however is found in services inflation. As seen in Exhibit 97 , services inflation, which represents 60% of the Swiss CPI basket has been sticky and is even showing signs of re-acceleration.

That being said, in February a print of +0.9%M will be dropping out of the observation period ( Exhibit 98 ), one of two prints that has optically been holding up the annual figure. There is therefore the chance that February will see services inflation joining the rest of the basket in a downward trend.

Exhibit 98: February 2023 saw particularly large monthly increase in the cost of services, which continues to hold up the annual figure
![](images/a17f2a884f6eec3beb803444635626d9f29ed621c3ecdf30262ae2f3ac72f8da.jpg)
Source: FSO, Morgan Stanley Researc

Exhibit 99: The SNB has underestimated inflation as it has risen and overestimated it as it has fallen
![](images/720891cd5f9a7b2b68beaf804c1a0e4242ddb5ba0e46ef70cc245a2ee8fd53f2.jpg)
Source: Swiss National Bank, Morgan Stanley Research

If therefore, all of the inflation metrics bar one (which is likely to fall in line in the near future) are moderating and the SNB's last t+1Q forecast was likely too aggressive ( Exhibit 99 ), why would the SNB not cut in March?

Because it doesn't need to.

The SNB's primary mandate is to ensure price stability, which it defines as headline yearover-year inflation between 0% and 2%. Currently, therefore, the SNB is comfortably achieving its target while remaining a significant distance away from falling out of the target range to the downside.

## What about growth?

The National Bank Act (NBA) complements the price stability target by adding that while ensuring price stability the SNB "shall take due account of economic developments".

However, based on recent economic data, the risks and potential damage from inflation still outweigh those from growth.

Real GDP grew by 1.1% on a seasonally-adjusted annualized basis in the 3rd quarter of 2023. The labour market is not showing signs of excess strain ( Exhibit 100 ), manufacturing surveys are improving again (despite still being contractionary) and services surveys remain expansionary ( Exhibit 101 ).

Exhibit 100:Unemployment in Switzerland has only increased marginally from 1.9% to 2.2%
![](images/6f011c25a032106a011457a06d9ade76a1fb8cb1bf9ae8057f4a180df151a46c.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 101:PMIs have improved from mid-2023 lows

Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Taken together therefore, the Swiss economy does not currently find itself in a particularly weak position. Growth may have been stronger in the past, but it does not yet require immediate support.

It will however, seemingly require support in the future. The Swiss State Secretariat for Economic Affairs (SECO), produces a high frequency (weekly) indicator of economic growth ( Exhibit 102 ) based on cash withdrawals, electricity consumption, air pollution, rail freight transport, card transactions, unemployment , sight deposits, goods exports, and goods imports.

While the Weekly Economic Activity (WEA) indicator implies we may see a small bump to Q4 GDP, the trend is clear. The WEA has been decreasing steadily and is now oscillating around the zero mark ( Exhibit 103 ).

![](images/6d6b92fa067c3b95d071ae1b5afbebd9cef5f6773cec3af5124e8d4edf587b8f.jpg)
Source: SECO, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 103:Recently the WEA has been trending downwards and looks to be indicating %Y GDP may soon come negative

![](images/49c96bd815435b675a51bf3585e583b7eafcf63b4b778a23f4422bcce05c95c4.jpg)
Source: SECO, Morgan Stanley Research

## The bottom line?

Despite inflation dynamics potentially allowing for a cut as early as March, growth doesn't yet require it. Hence, the SNB may opt to wait for June. Ensuring by doing so that price pressures continue to decline.

Unless, we see rapid deterioration of economic conditions or increased downward momentum in inflation suggesting Switzerland may soon have a deflation problem, risks are tilted to the first cut in June rather than March.

# JPY | Is there a risk of potential MoF intervention?

MORGAN STANLEY MUFG SECURITIES CO., LTD.

## Koichi Sugisaki

Koichi.Sugisaki@morganstanleymufg.com

+81 3 6836-8428

USD/JPY has climbed back above 150 amid renewed demand for JPY carry trades as US short-term r\* has been repriced sharply higher (see Exhibit 104 ) following the unexpectedly strong CPI print for January (with services inflation the main driver).

Markets have started to wonder about the possibility of JPY-supportive intervention, with Vice Finance Minister for International Affairs Masato Kanda on February 14 characterizing the JPY’s slide so far this year as “pretty rapid” and declaring that some of the recent moves “are clearly speculative” (and hence “undesirable”).

JPY depreciation since January does indeed appear to have gone beyond what can be explained by differences in the monetary policy stances of the Fed and BoJ (see Exhibit 105 ). Some have attributed this to flows associated with Japan’s recently enhanced NISA (Nippon Individual Savings Account) tax-free investment scheme, but as we discussed in "Impact Of The “New NISA” Tax-Free Investment Scheme", other than in the second week of January domestic investors do not appear to have been making particularly significant allocations to overseas equities and investment trusts (see Exhibit 106 ).

Exhibit 104:Higher US short-term r\* pricing has led to higher USD/JPY led by USD strength
![](images/762698357b492cecc6a9dc290b1c42142103c63f7ae33ba49c3706ef6ab9266e.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Exhibit 105:USD/JPY still remains rich vs implied fair value
![](images/b97b80a89db6633cea380ba1c21105c1474cb6ef44d64ad651539a404e5494bd.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

CFTC positioning data point to asset managers (in particular) increasing their JPY-short exposure of late, which suggests to us that MoF officials might indeed have good reason to worry about a buildup of speculative positioning for on further JPY depreciation (see Exhibit 107 ).

Exhibit 106:Net inflows to foreign equity/funds from Japanese investors decreased after the second week of January

![](images/d41781d795847247b31f362c9a5986498f538c36fe957f05d2e81037ab98806c.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Japan MoF

Exhibit 107:Asset managers have flipped from JPY long to JPY short since new year start
![](images/25477add1ea83f93d7acb7927bcaf3b664ec3c769e9d79f4c98142eba484c5b6.jpg)
Source: CFTC Morgan Stanley Research

But the MoF is only likely to be concerned by the speed at which the JPY has been sliding, with currency weakness itself probably not considered all that problematic. The previous intervention in September 2022 came as Japan faced a significant deterioration in its terms of trade (see Exhibit 108 ) due to a surge in contract-currency import prices (reflecting pandemic-related supply constraints). This, coupled with marked JPY depreciation, led to a steady acceleration in domestic inflation via higher import costs (see Exhibit 109 ).

Exhibit 108:In September 2022, Japan's terms of trade (contract currency basis) had worsened notably, with broad JPY weakness.
![](images/58a051766f25fb14e9bd90892c2bc958b0ff02348ff4adfcbd729a5fde0b41f0.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, BoJ

Exhibit 109:Higher import costs (JPY denominated) led to higher CPI inflation (YoY changes)
![](images/e104a55c4457834263bd8bc6b65374fdaf7633467e6a861cc82a7119a0dced88.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, BoJ, Macrobonds

USD/JPY has since moved above 150 in November and more recently, but contractcurrency import prices are down from their peak levels, as a result of which the terms of trade have improved significantly compared to when JPY-supportive intervention was deemed necessary back in 2022 ( Exhibit 108 , Exhibit 109 ). The pace of domestic CPI has meanwhile slowed as JPY-denominated import prices remain comparatively grounded.

Moreover, the JPY’s weakness is now serving to boost exporter profits and thereby improve the prospects of annual labor-management pay negotiations, culminating in sizable wage hikes for a second straight year. Our suspicion is thus that the Kishida administration—having pledged to boost income growth beyond the pace of inflation— will be in no particular hurry to curb the JPY’s slide and thereby risk depressing corporate profits.

Any eventual intervention decision is thus likely to be made in response to JPY depreciation that is deemed “excessive”—such as if USD/JPY surges sharply higher in reaction to (say) stronger-than-expected US economic data or hints by BoJ officials that an exit from the negative interest rate policy might be deferred—rather than because any particular exchange rate has crossed a certain threshold. Conversely, we see little prospect of the MoF stepping in if the JPY simply continues to move gradually lower amid demand for (JPY-short) carry trades.

The risk of US short-term r\* being repriced sharply higher appears to have diminished in the wake of the unexpectedly weak January retail sales report (which also saw figures for November and December revised downwards) and the resultant rethink of the probability of sticky services inflation coexisting with robust demand.

That said, it would hardly be all that surprising to see further demand for JPY carry trades given that US-Japan interest rate differentials as yet look likely to keep widening for at least the time being.

So what might it take to turn the tide? Probably something along the lines of markets starting to price in a “hard landing” scenario. But when might that happen? Timing is of course very difficult to predict.

However, the risk of such a turn of events currently appears to be greatest in Europe, where the economy continues to struggle and market participants might be inclined to price in a more dovish ECB rate cut trajectory depending on how inflation tracks from here.

If markets see a risk of a hard landing in the euro area, this could potentially be enough to trigger a rapid correction of euro-long/JPY-short positioning, for which reason we as yet suggest staying short EUR/JPY in anticipation of “hard landing” risk being upwardly repriced.

• Trade idea: Maintain short EUR/JPY at 161.84 with a target of 145 and a stop of 162

## United States

# United States | Breakevens enjoy the inflation tailwind

MORGAN STANLEY & CO. LLC

Guneet Dhingra, CFA

## Strong CPI strong breakevens

The week was headlined by a strong inflation print, with especially strong contribution from services inflation. With the tailwind of strong labor market optics two weeks ago, breakevens have continued to do well on a vanilla basis as well as beta-adjusted basis (see Exhibit 111 ). It helped that oil prices rallied over the week, boosted by a stronger demand picture and continued geopolitical tensions.

Exhibit 110:Moves in real yields and breakevens in the past week
![](images/2883c743d14efa17d38914d5a7ee6ad6918d1895802cdfca773671695105177f.jpg)

Exhibit 111:Moves in vanilla and beta-adjusted breakevens in the last six months
![](images/fb6bab2a69fb803c266ca8d0e05b0a9cf16a70450cee359023b20ac6f71dfce9.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

As we note in the US duration section (see United States | Seasonal supremacy sways yields ), the upside in January core CPI has become a bit of trend - 11 of the last 12 years - likely explained by residual seasonality. No wonder then that breakevens have widened in 8 of the last 10 February months, and 6 of the last 7 (the exception being 2020) - see ExhibitG 112 .

While inflation looked strong, ETF inflows in the TIPS space look pretty lukewarm, suggesting a continued lack of excitement about the TIPS product - see Exhibit 113 .

Overall, while residual seasonality and oil uptick has helped brekevens widen, we do not see a sustainable path for breakevens to keep widening, given the medium term outlook for cooling inflation, and the vulnerability of breakevens to hard-landing-type outcomes, while although unlikely, can keep breakevens cheap. We stay neutral on breakevens for now.

![](images/339309cee92a321f72d3d9822a7218679a872c1294e73e2573fdec97edbc6bed.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 113:TIPS ETF rolling 3 month inflows
![](images/8277605b26a8628fcd348c0e74800168c2cb757b788af78b311bea209ed7b5eb.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

## January CPI recap – strong, with help from residual seasonality?

January core CPI came in at 0.39%, well above our expectation and Bloomberg median. The uptick comes from almost all service categories, while core goods came in negative, helped by used cars (-3.37%). Within services, rents continued to cool with the revised CPI trend, but core services ex-shelter was the strongest since April 2022.

Rent and OER – progress…and lack of it: Rent and OER showed contrasting pictures. Rents continued to cool, in line with the decline we saw post the CPI revisions. On the other hand, OER showed an uptick in what could be called a re-acceleration - see Exhibit 126 . Importantly, OER can be more volatile than rent because it adjusts for rent by subtracting the cost of utilities, which can be inherently volatile (and utility costs have been falling for last few months - see United States | Seasonal supremacy sways yields .

In the big picture, the New Tenant Rent (NTR) shows new tenant rents falling below the pre-pandemic levels –a negative sign for shelter inflation over coming months (see Exhibit 115 ).

Powell’s closely-watched category – a very strong uptick in December: Core services, ex-shelter, moved to 0.85% in January – the highest print since April 2022 as the volatile categories like hotels, airfares, as well as car insurance picked up. The 3m and 6m trend is 5.55%, and 6.70%, respectively - well above the 12m trend. The re-acceleration in this category is coming from all sectors.

![](images/dead56b0b1745ed79e50be20d2a6e981ae5e8a36d97577438d4c86330771016e.jpg)

![](images/0862cfaf60dd1628844465e16feaf865119acab3d5a5a7ac94d228330faf4de1.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Healthcare services has been getting a major boost from healthcare insurance reset (almost 4bp a month addition to core), and there also seems to be an element of a catchup in hospital services in line with healthcare wage pick-up from last year, which had been expected to feed through.

Trimmed means and medians – a notable uptick: The trimmed mean CPI in January came in around 0.49%, well above the trailing 6m average of \~0.27% as services picked up broadly. In five of the last seven January months including 2024, there seems to be an uptick with a local maximum in January. Median CPI print came in at 0.52% m/m in January - well higher than the 6m average 0.34%.

Car prices back to deflation: Used car prices fell 3.37% in January, a little lower than suggested by the Manheim data. Manheim data suggests that used car prices could keep move lower going forward, and our auto analysts too expect further normalization. New car prices were unchanged in January (-0.04% m/m).

PCE update - more core PCE progress: Incorporating core CPI and PPI, our economists expect core PCE to rise by 0.43% m/m in January – which will negatively affect the increased confidence on inflation sought by the Fed.

## Short-Duration Strategy

## United States

MMFs take a pause in extending: WAM has remained around 38 days as front-end investors re-assess the timing and pace of eventual Fed cuts. As expected, this shift in Fed expectations has led MMFs to be more patient before extending out further. WAM is 3 days higher vs. December 2021 (a couple of months before the start of the hiking cycle) after more than doubling since early last year. In addition, this pause in MMF extension has implications for funding conditions as it leaves more cash in the very front-end.

Rates sell-off contributes to soft funding: Stable MMF WAM has also coincided with a plateau in the demand for repo funding. This has been mainly driven by the year-to-date rates sell off (10y UST +40bp YTD), which has, as expected, aligned with a moderation in SOFR volumes and has also contributed to soft repo rates.

Maintain short 2y swap spreads ahead of catalyst for tighter funding conditions: We continue to expect tighter funding conditions ahead driven by 1) larger UST supply, 2) a sustained decline in the RRP and an eventual increase in the TGA due to April tax receipts, and 3) greater bank liquidity needs given ongoing deposit competition and higher funding costs. We maintain short 2y (Jan '26) swap spreads at -11.3bp.

To extend or not to extend

Money market fund (MMF) weighted average maturity (WAM) peaked at 38.8 days two weeks ago ( Exhibit 116 ). Since then, WAM has remained around 38 days as front-end investors re-assess the timing and pace of eventual Fed cuts.

![](images/f2b3480c080d82af027f3e0f53296cdd6f36c755141d94d1f0f1aea200aa82b6.jpg)
Source: Crane Data, Morgan Stanley Research

![](images/78e5cfa79d02b63569c4d1c63ba0ad2e3edbbfcc7a59cfd734df8f0e09e796df.jpg)
Source: Crane Data, Morgan Stanley Research

The week-over-week change in WAM has steadily declined ( Exhibit 117 ) and has remained close to flat for the past 2 weeks as market expectations for cuts over the next three FOMC meetings have significantly declined. Month-to-date, the amount of cumulative cuts priced in for the June FOMC has declined from 2.4 to 0.9 (see Exhibit 118 ).

As expected, this shift in Fed expectations has led MMFs to be more patient before extending further. Relative to recent years, WAM is 3 days higher vs. December 2021 (a couple of months before the start of the hiking cycle) after more than doubling since early last year ( Exhibit 119 ).

In addition, this pause in MMF extension has implications for funding conditions as it leaves more cash in the very front end. As MMFs become cautious, they are more willing to lend in repo (1-day duration) as they wait for a better entry point to keep extending via longer duration assets (bills).

Exhibit 118:Cuts priced in for the next 3 FOMC meetings
![](images/e97e82a9d27c5ae2f12509f3f595a5ade96d884b0656adaf52a4a28bde3df4f6.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

## Rates sell-off contributes to soft funding conditions

Stable MMF WAM has also coincided with a plateau in the demand for repo funding. This has been mainly driven by the year-to-date rates sell-off (10y UST +40bp YTD), which has, as expected, aligned with moderation in SOFR volumes (proxy for demand for repo financing).

As shown in Exhibit 120 , the direction of rates has helped inform trends in SOFR volume over the past year. The relationship is expected as repo volume is measured in fair value terms. In addition, higher rates also tend to lead to a smaller net long position in the market and potentially the emergence of a short base. For instance, on-the-run 2y and 5y notes have traded special this week as demand from shorts picks up.

Exhibit 120:SOFR volume and BBG UST Total Return Index
![](images/35de997f8bea2cf2b0c2f2e1a47430f2f4a445df3b9493a2593ba1a90882055f.jpg)
Source: Federal Reserve, Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 121:SOFR volume and asset manager net UST futures holdings
![](images/899b4b8f61cd413d2a654a5af93b23efb11b93e6f2f9757f21d769d3b07fdbd6.jpg)
Source: Federal Reserve, CFTC, Morgan Stanley Research

In the same vein, the moderation in SOFR volume has also coincided with stable net long futures holdings by asset managers as shown in Exhibit 139 Exhibit 121 . This also limits further increases in the demand for repo financing via the cash-futures basis trade.

More cash in the front end as MMFs take a pause on extending and a moderation in the demand for repo financing continues to contribute to soft funding conditions. This was evidenced in SOFR printing at 530bp (same as the RRP rate) on 2/14 for the first time since October. As a result, market expectations for future funding conditions have returned to 3Q23 levels (see Exhibit 122 ).

Exhibit 122:3m, 6m and 1y SOFR/FF
![](images/35c0b6ad327922f463dbc1f3e911deab2e6300f17ffc4bebee6a948d30e631a9.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 123:T-bill net settlements
![](images/1d519fa0c3cd63373ad781d4879fd203bef3d92229418545da96b680bcf9b85d.jpg)
Source: US Treasury, Morgan Stanley Research

As we discussed recently, we continue to expect tighter funding conditions ahead driven by larger UST supply ( Exhibit 123 ), 2) a sustained decline in the RRP and an eventual increase in the TGA due to April tax receipts, and 3) greater bank liquidity needs given ongoing deposit competition and higher funding costs. We maintain short 2y (Jan '26) swap spreads at -11.3bp.

Over the coming weeks, a continued sell-off in front-end rates could likely keep 2y swap spreads biased wider and we maintain our stop of -9bp. At the same token, an potential rally should contribute to tighten 2y swap spreads as demand to pay 2y SOFR moderates and demand for repo financing resumes its climb higher along with more UST supply.

## Maintain short 2y swap spreads ahead of catalysts for tighter funding conditions

Stable funding conditions year-to-date have also been evidenced in the RRP balance itself, which remains \$19bn higher over the past two weeks (2/16 vs. 2/2). As shown Exhibit 124 , the number of RRP users has increased by 14 week-over-week to a year-to-date high of 94. Some of this increase is likely explained by GSEs as MBS pay downs are distributed over the coming days.

A lower desire to extend paired with steady inflows into MMFs has created an environment conducive for a more steady decline in the RRP. As shown in Exhibit 125 , total MMF AUM made another new record high of \$6.43tr this week, while the pace of growth has moderated recently, coming in at \$9bn WoW as of 2/15.

![](images/59994855a9dfa2ac668ef1d44d79e242656d5fb93422f43bd1277430d1d9b302.jpg)
Source: Federal Reserve, Morgan Stanley Research

![](images/dd9f7937f711938ffa909d929fb3b2d9d7398762970cfcaa747ee9e7409a7bef.jpg)
Source: Crane Data, Morgan Stanley Research

Given that an RRP depletion will coincide with a repricing higher in SOFR as MMFs no longer act as the marginal lenders in repo (willing to lend at any rate above the RRP) and IORB becomes the new anchor (banks will lend reserves in repo at rates above IORB), a negative SOFR/FF spread (SOFR above EFFR) should coincide with a funding environment in which the RRP is depleted.

As shown in Exhibit 126 , market pricing based on 1-month SOFR and fed funds futures suggest that the RRP will be depleted in May-June. This keeps funding risks concentrated in 2H24 and onwards ( Exhibit 127 ) as bank reserves start to decline in line with QT. This also continues to inform our expectation that the Fed will taper QT starting in June (see here for more).

Exhibit 126:1m SOFR/FF futures contracts
![](images/68e582e525ab0c7634e4505aa092d5aacd2698d12331cf30c0f9bb59836fa9ee.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 127:SOFR - IORB spread and RRP usage
![](images/14a9685f36267258f8ae678675de05ddb45665c8c41a15ac749dc8cd8d539e34.jpg)
Source: Federal Reserve, Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Looking ahead, we continue to see the RRP depleted by June due to the catalysts discussed above. First, ongoing UST will allow SOFR to start climbing gradually higher from this week's recent low of 530bp. In particular, month-end UST settlements will be significantly over February month-end and quarter-end relative to January.

Second, as shown in Exhibit 128 , we expect the TGA to surpass \$1tr in April due to tax receipts. This could lead the RRP to be below \$200bn by early May. As we have highlighted in our 2024 outlook, reserves have remained sticky as banks maintain high liquidity levels. This has led the RRP to absorb all of the liquidity drain generated by ongoing QT and the TGA rebuild since June 2023.

We can illustrate this dynamic in Exhibit 129 by noting that over the past year the RRP has maintained a very close relationship with the TGA, typically declining as the TGA increases further while reserves have remained large stable above \$3tr. This leads us to expect the RRP to absorb most of the TGA surge this coming April as higher repo rates and higher bank demand for funding keep the RRP steadily declining ( Exhibit 129 ).

In addition, MMFs tend to see outflows when April tax payments are made. In 2022, daily Crane Data shows that MMF AUM declined by \~\$100bn week-over-week on the tax payment deadline. In 2023, the decline was \$40bn week-over-week as higher yields and lower capital gain taxes due reduced the decline. A dip in MMF AUM in 2H April will likely be reflected in a lower RRP balance.

Third, marginal bank liquidity needs will likely remain positive given ongoing deposit competition. As shown in Exhibit 130 , time deposits and borrowings continue to make a larger share of total liabilities in recent weeks. In addition, the distribution of reserves remains unequal when looking at the amount of reserves held by large (top 25) US banks vs. small ( Exhibit 131 ).

• Trade idea: Maintain short 2y (Jan '26) SOFR swap spreads at -11.2bp with a stop of -9bp

## Interest Rate Derivatives

## United States

More gamma vs. vega divergence: Upper left vols were relatively flat on the week, while vega moved upwards. Vega vol is nearing 1y highs and screens rich versus the rest of the surface on an implied/realized basis.

Buying downside rates distribution: We think (1) the week's shift in the distribution of rates away from levels consistent with our economic outlook, (2) receiver skew richness, and (3) 2y tail RV have made owning the downside distribution of 2y rates attractive. We recommend investors enter long 1x2x1 3m2y receiver butterfly (ATMF-10/ATMF-30/ATMF-60).

Analyzing WN wild-card fair value: The fair value of the WN contract's wild-card option has been topical this week as we approach first delivery. We examine what swaption volatility suggests the wildcard option value should be.

Swaptions have re-distributed downside risks to rates into upside risks to rates. This can be seen when analyzing the w/w change in swaption-implied 3m-terminal distribution of 2y rates (see Exhibit 138 ). The market is pricing a \~8% chance of 2y rates ending in the 5- 5.5% range in 3m - a material deviation from fair value, under our economists' outlook for policy rates.

Receiving skew is rich, as a bid for left-tail protection keeps out-of-the-money vols elevated relative to ATMF vols (see Exhibit 139 ). Indeed, 3m2y ATMF-50bp receivers are trading with norms \~8% higher than ATMF receivers. This number is on the upper range observed since 2022 (see Exhibit 140 ).

Exhibit 138: Swaption-implied terminal distribution of 2y rates in 3m
![](images/09a34a5dfffb6611a8812a52e9a42356bfd05f2a27bb510f79af420441bcdcb6.jpg)

Vols on 2y tails are optically high, but are on the lower end of their 1y lookback. Moreover, on a implied/realized (I/R) basis, implieds have lagged moves in underlyings over the past three months (see Exhibit 141 ), suggesting gamma vol is not particularly rich given the current rate environment.

Additionally, I/R ratios on 2y tails are low relative to the rest of the surface, suggesting RV cheapness versus other points.

We think (1) the shift in the distribution of rates away from levels consistent with our economic outlook, (2) receiver skew richness, and (3) the relative cheapness of 2y swaption tails on the surface have made owning the downside distribution of 2y rates attractive. We thus recommend investors enter into long 1x2x1 receiver butterfly via:

• Long \$300mm ATMF-10bp 3m2y receiver

• Short \$600mm ATMF-35bp 3m2y receiver

• Long \$300mm ATMF-60bp 3m2y receiver

This structure costs \~\$157k up front, and is meant to be held to expiry. The main risk to the trade is a continued sell-off in rates, leading the options to expire OTM and thus lose the premium.

## Valuing the WN Wild-card Option with 1m30y Implied Vol

The WN futures contract is unique in that it contains a sizeable wild-card option that can result in a large net basis at delivery. For example, the WNH4 cheapest-to-deliver's (CTD) net basis to 1-Mar-24 is over 9 ticks. Net basis generally consists of a switch option (the option of which underlying bond to deliver) and a timing option (the option of when in the delivery month to deliver). As delivery approaches, the value of the switch option diminishes as the final yield curve becomes more certain. The net basis that remains can be primarily explained by timing options, and in the particular, the wild card.

The value of the wild-card option comes from afternoon changes in the deliverable bond price, specifically from 3 p.m. (when the futures market closes for the day) and 7 p.m. (when the short position must decide whether to deliver into the futures).

The reason for this is that every day during the delivery period, someone long the basis – long 1 unit of the bond and short a number futures contracts equal to the conversion factor (C) – faces a choice:

1. Sell the basis position, collecting the basis at that point in time (in practice, this choice is restricted by low futures liquidity during the delivery month).

2. Deliver C units of the bond into the futures contract, then sell the remaining 1 – C bonds, referred to as the delivery tail.

3. Hold the basis position to the next day and collect a day's worth of carry.

If the person long basis chooses to deliver (option 2), then they lose the value of the basis. However, if the size of the tail, 1 – C, times the bond price increases by more than the value of the basis during the 3-7 p.m. period, then they profit by delivering into the futures, and they have until 7 p.m., after they have seen the bond price change, to decide whether to deliver. The value of the wild card is thus determined by the expected sizes of these afternoon price changes (i.e., price volatility), the conversion factor, which determines the size of the delivery tail, and carry – the cost of holding the wild-card option to the next day.

We examine whether 1m30y implied volatility, which could be a proxy for the expected size of afternoon CTD price changes during the delivery month, explains the value of the wild-card option. In Exhibit 142 , we regress net basis at First Intention Day on 1 – C times implied volatility over all 56 past WN delivery cycles, from 2010 to 2023. We multiply implied vol by 1 – C because the value of the delivery tail is proportional to the conversion factor.

Exhibit 142:WN net basis (in dollars) at First Intention Day vs. 1m30y implied volatility times delivery tail size (1 – C)
![](images/c36a74dccaa10c03f6aac93d7b9602a7b54673e03ddc383df1b149a737c48b68.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Exhibit 143:Regression results: WN net basis vs. 1m30y implied volatility times delivery tail size (1 – C)

<table><tr><td>Coefficien</td><td>t-statistic t</td><td>p-value</td></tr><tr><td>1m30y vol times tail</td><td>0.013 -8.01</td><td>0.00</td></tr><tr><td>size</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Constant</td><td>-0.173 14.44</td><td>0.00</td></tr><tr><td>R-squared</td><td>0.794</td><td></td></tr></table>

Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Exhibit 142 and Exhibit 143 show that 1m30y implied vol explains a large part of wildcard option pricing, with a regression R-squared of 79%. Each unit of implied vol times, scaled by the tail size, is worth 1.3 cents of net basis. Furthermore, our regression implies that the WNH4 CTD net basis is currently trading cheap to fair value: 29 cents vs. 33 cents (9.375 ticks vs. 10.625 ticks).

This analysis leads us to a similar conclusion that we arrived at in our analysis of WN in the quarterly Futures Rolls Report: that the WNH4 contract looks rich (basis is cheap) when accounting for the wild-card option. While in the rolls report, we used price changes during past delivery months to value the WN wild card; in this analysis, we look forward to implied vol over the coming month, giving us a different – and possibly more accurate – assessment of optionality.

• Trade idea: Enter 1x2x1 3m2y receiver butterfly (ATMF-10/ATMF-30/ ATMF-60) trade @ 3bp running

## Pivot Points

Pivot points are charting levels used by day traders to determine market direction, support, and resistance levels. We calculate weekly pivot points using the previous week's open, high, low, and closing levels.

In The Tactical Bull Market Is Back, we discussed a simple methodology based on the Ichimoku Kinko charting technique for classifying market movements as bullish, bearish, or range bound. Then, we define whether the market movement is cyclical or secular in nature. A cyclical move is shorter term in nature, and a secular move is longer term in nature. For cyclical moves, we further divide them into tactical and strategic. We use daily data to inform tactical moves, and weekly data to inform strategic moves. We use monthly data to inform secular movements.

# G4 Smarter (beta) Trading Strategy

MORGAN STANLEY & CO. LLC

Matthew Hornbach, CMT

Matthew.Hornbach@morganstanley.com

+1 212 761-1837

Enhancements to a G4 10y government bond futures momentum strategy have produced higher Sharpe ratios and stronger returns, relative to total return government bond indices for the G4, US, Germany, Japan, and the UK since 2000. See A "Smarter" (Beta) Way to Trade G4 10y Futures Duration? for more information on these strategies.

## Trading Strategy 1 – "Trade Longs/Fade Shorts"

When the 5-day moving average crosses above the 20-day moving average, buy the futures contract (long duration) and hold for a 25-business-day period. When the 5-day moving average crosses below the 20-day moving average, buy the futures contract and hold for a 25-business-day period. In short, this strategy buys futures when the Simple Moving Average Crossover (SMAX) generates both a long and a short signal, given the historical outperformance of long signals traded long and underperformance of short signals traded short. Given that the SMAX could generate both a long and a short signal within the predefined holding period, an investor may have a 200% long position since each of the two signals would be traded in separate portfolio sleeves.

## Trading Strategy 2 – Trade "Longs Only"

When the 5-day moving average crosses above the 20-day moving average, buy the futures contract (long duration) and hold for a 25-business-day period. When the 5-day moving average crosses below the 20-day moving average, do nothing. In short, an investor ONLY trades long signals initiated by the SMAX given their historical precedent to outperform

## Bond Market Indicators

Our BMI(10) models are neutral to bearish for all regions. Vol-adjusted carry is bearish for all regions, while momentum is bearish for the U.K. and Canada. Equity market signals are bearish for the U.S. and Japan, while business cycle indicators are bullish for the U.S., Japan, and Australia. FX signals are bullish for Germany, Japan, and New Zealand.

Our BMI(2) models are bullish for Japan and neutral to bearish otherwise. Voladjusted carry is bullish for Japan only, while momentum is bullish for Australia only. Equity market signals and business cycle indicators are the same as for our BMI(10) models. FX signals are bearish for the U.S., the U.K., and Australia.

Our iBMI models are bullish for TIPS & HICPxt and neutral for other regions. Oil signal grew more bullish for TIPS & UKTi, remained as bullish for HICPxT, and grew less bullish for JGBi. Momentum signal turned bearish for JGBi and grew less bearish for all other regions. Equities signal turned bullish for TIPS, remained as bearish for UKTi, grew less bearish for HICPxT and grew less bullish for JGBi. Value signal grew less bullish for TIPS, remained as bullish for UKTi & HICPxT, and grew less bearish for JGBi.

## Swap Spread Indicators

Our SSI(2) models imply that 2y spreads are roughly 29.5bp wide to fair value on a 6m rolling lookback. The 1std threshold is met. Our model-implied fair value can be found on Bloomberg using the ticker MSSIUS2 Index.

Our SSI(10) models imply that 10y spreads are 53.1bp tight on a 6m rolling lookback. The 1std trading threshold is met. Our model-implied fair value can be found on Bloomberg using the ticker MSSIUS10 Index.

Our SSI(30) models suggest that 30y spreads are 74.5bp tight to fair value on our 2y lookback window. The 2std threshold is met. Our model-implied fair value can be found on Bloomberg using the ticker MSSIUS30 Index.

Based on each of the SSI models, the 2s10s spread curve is \~82.6bp flat to fair value using a 6m lookback. The 10s30s spread curve is about \~51.3bp flat to fair value using our 2y lookback window.

Detail on the variable selection and model construction of these Swap Spread Indicators can be found in Modeling Swap Spreads. Within the piece, we discuss the various fundamental and flow-related drivers of 2y, 10y, and 30y spreads, and use these variables to construct multivariate regression models. We then develop and test trading strategies that employ these models. Updates to model-implied fair values, as well as backtesting of trading signals, can be found below.

## Government Bond Supply

In the US, new 20y UST will be issued for \$16bn and new 30y TIPS, for \$9bn. These will settle in the week after next when \$15bn coupons and \$135.8bn redemptions will be paid. In the euro area, we estimate €15.5bn of issuance against €1.1bn coupons and no redemptions. In the UK, UKT 4% Gilt 2063 will be issued for £1.75bn and UKT 4.5% Gilt 2028 for £4bn, against no coupons and no redemptions coming into the market. In Japan, a 20y JGB will be issued for ¥1000bn and there will be an auction for enhanced liquidity for ¥500bn. This is against ¥4.8bn coupons and no redemptions coming into the market. In Canada, 10y CAN 3% Jun 2034 will be issued for \$5bn against no coupons and no redemptions coming into the market. In Australia, ACGB 3.75% April 2037 will be issued for \$800m against \$0.1bn coupons and no redemptions. In New Zealand, NZGBs will be issued for an estimated \$0.5bn against no cash flow coming into the market.

## In Case You Missed It

## UK Inflation Watch: Weighing the Details

## 14 Feb 2024

Headline and core missed relative to consensus and the BoE's forecasts. The January print is always complex, and this was no exception. That said, while weights and hotel prices blur the picture, on any reasonable measure of core pressures, this was a soft print. We stick with the May cut.

## Global Futures Rolls Report: US Treasury and Gilt Futures Rolls

## 13 Feb 2024

With global central banks clearly moving towards a policy tightening cycle, in contrast to the BoJ, the JPY rates market will likely continue to take direction from overseas. We stay bearish on both duration and JPY.

## Global Macro Strategy: Global FX Positioning: Short JPY Positioning at Highest Level Since October

## 12 Feb 2024

In the week ending Friday, February 9, options pricing data indicate that investors added long NZD and SEK (versus EUR) positions, and increased short JPY and GBP positions.In the futures market, investors bought NZD and USD (DXY), while most USD longs were added against EUR and CHF in the week ending Tuesday, February 6.

## Podcast | Global: The Global Macro Guide: February 9

## 9 Feb 2024

We review the supply, coupon and redemption picture for the US, UK, Euro, JPY, AUS, NZ, CAN and CNY government bond markets over the next month. This week, G7 net DV01 to be -\$34m/bp, compared to an average\* \$71.3m/bp. US -\$56.8m/bp; Euro \$18.6m/bp; UK \$5.9m/bp.

## UK Fiscal Policy: Delaying the Inevitable

## 8 Feb 2024

For March, we put fiscal space at £23 billion and assume \~£15 billion of easing/year, largely via (income) tax cuts. We assume tax cuts will be largely reversed post-November to fund the likely Spending Review increase in current expenditure. In the long run, the fiscal picture looks challenging if rates remain structurally higher.

## Definition of terms

Buy/Long: The analyst expects the total or excess return (depending on the nature of the recommendation) of the instrument or issuer that is the subject of the investment recommendation to be positive over the relevant time period.

Sell/Short: The analyst expects the total or excess return (depending on the nature of the recommendation) of the instrument or issuer that is the subject of the investment recommendation to be negative over the relevant time period.

Selling protection or Buying Risk: The analyst expects that the price of protection against the event occurring will decrease over the relevant time period.

Buying protection or Selling Risk: The analyst expects the price of protection against the event occurring will increase over the relevant time period.

Pay: The analyst expects that over the specified time period the variable rate underlying the swap agreement that is the subject of the investment recommendation will increase.

Receive: The analyst expects that over the specified time period the variable rate underlying the swap agreement that is the subject of the investment recommendation will decrease.

Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for recommendations included in the Morgan Stanley Fixed Income Research reports is 1 - 3 months and the price of financial instruments mentioned in the recommendation is as at the date and time of publication of the recommendation.

When more than one issuer or instrument is included in a recommendation, analyst expects one part of the trade to outperform the other trade or combination of other trades included in the recommendation on a relative basis.

For important disclosures related to the proportion of all investment recommendations over the past 12 months that fit each of the categories defined above, and the proportion of issuers corresponding to each of those categories to which Morgan Stanley has supplied material services, please see the Morgan Stanley disclosure at https:// ny.matrix.ms.com/eqr/article/webapp/45053cc2-ab1b-11ee-95af-a1eee2204a2c

## STOCK RATINGS

Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy, Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell. Investors should carefully read the definitions of all ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone. In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice. An investor's decision to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

## Analyst Stock Ratings

Overweight (O or Over) - The stock's total return is expected to exceed the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months.

Equal-weight (E or Equal) - The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months.

Not-Rated (NR) - Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Underweight (U or Under) - The stock's total return is expected to be below the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months

## Analyst Industry Views

Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.
